lton Deficiency 
9 Three Stages of iron deficiency are 

(i) First stage is characterized by decreased storage of iron without any detectable abnormalities. 
(ii) An intermediate stage of "latent iron deficiency", that is, iron stores are exhausted, but anaemia has not} 
| occurred yet. 

(iii) The third stage is that of overt iron deficiency when there is a decrease in concentration of circulating 
haemoglobin due to impaired haemoglobin synthesis. 

° ~ Theend result of iron deficiency is nutritional anaemia, impaired cell mediated immunity, reduce resistance 
to infection, increased morbidity and mortality and diminished work performance. 

2 Cut off for diagnosis of Anaemia (WHO) 

'A dult males 13 g/dl 
Adult females (non pregnant) 12 g/dl 
Adult female (pregnant) 11 g/dl 
Children (6m-6y) 11 g/dl 
Children (6y-14y) < 12 g/dl

Goitre Control 

There are four essential components of National Iodine deficiency disorder control programme; these 
include. 

(i) lodized Salt 

© The iodization of salt is now the most widely used prophylactic public health measure against endemic 
goitre. 

@ Level of iodization: 30 ppm at production level and 15 ppm at consumer level. 

Iodized salt is most economical, convenient and effective mean of mass prophylaxis in endemic areas. 

lodized oil : An intramuscular injection of 1 ml of iodized oil (Most poppy seed oil) will provide 

protection for about 4 years. 

(ii) —4odine monitoring 
Ls 

| Neonatal hypothyroidism is a sensitive pointer to environmental iodine deficiency and can thus be an 

effective indicator for monitoring the impact of a programme. 

Iodine monitoring is done through iodine excretion determination, determination of iodinei n water, 

soil wn food as part of epidemiological studies and determination of iodinei n salt for quality control. 

(ili) Manpower Training . ie 

[tis vital for the success of control that health workers and others engaged in the programme be fully tr
aine 

in all aspects of goitre control including legal enforcement & public education
. 

(iv) Mass Communication | | ie 

It is a powerful tool for nutrition education; creation of public awareness is central 
issue of a success 

| public health programme.

e  Riceis the staple food of more than half of the human race. 
e Rice grain consists of 3 parts - the germ (embryo), the inner endosperm, and the outer pericarp and aleurone 

grain layer. 

» The endosperm is composed mostly of starch. 
° ‘Riceisa good source of B-group Vitamins, especially thiamine. 
e __ Rice is devoid of Vitamin A, D and C. 

e _ Rice is a poor source of calcium & iron. 

Effect of Milling 

e The milling process causes loss of proteins upto 15%, 75% of thiamine and 60% of riboflavin and niacin. 

People subsisting mainly on white or polished rice are prone to beri-beri. 

|\Washing and Cooking 

Washing in large quantities of water would remove upto 60% of water soluble Vitamins & Minerals. 

Cooking rice in large quantifies of water and draining away the excess of water at the end of cooking leads 

to further loss of B group Vitamins. 

|Parboiling 

Parboiling (partial cooking in steam) is ancient Indian technique of preserving the nutritive q
uality of rice. 

Recommended technique is Hot soaking process. 

Procedure : Soak paddy (Unhusked rice) in hot water (65-70°C) for 3-4 hour
s 

J 
Drain the water, steam the soaked paddy for 5-10 min 

J 
Paddy is dried and later home pounded/ Milled 

Uses 

Nutrients in outer layer go into the inside layer and get 
firmly attached. 

Hardened rice more resistant to insect invasion. 

More suitable for storage. 

Improves keeping quality. 
; Disadvantages 

Peculiar smell, taste may develop.

ENERGY 

Energy is a prime requisite for body function and growth. 

e Total energy requirement of an individual is made up of three components 

(i) Energy required for basal metabolism. (1 kcal/hr/kg body wt of adult) 

(ii) Energy required for daily activities such as walking, sitting, standing, dressing etc. 

(iiii) Energy expenditure for occupational work.

BALANCED DIET CM 5.4 
F 

It is defined as one which contains a variety ood Guide Pyramid for balanced diet for 2000 Kcal 

of foods in such quantities and proportions 
that the need for energy, amino acids, 

Vitamins, Minerals, Fats, Carbohydrates 

and other nutrients is adequately met for 

maintaining health, Vitality and general 

well being and includes a small provision of 

extra nutrients to tide over emergencies. 

¢ Composition of Balanced Diet 

(i) Proteins should constitute 10-15% of 

total daily energy intake. 

(ii) Fats should constitute 15-30% of total 

daily energy intake. 

(iii) Carbohydrates constitutes remaining 

50-70% of energy. 

® "The food guide pyramid" emphasizes foods from the five major food groups shown in the t
hree lower 

sections of pyramid; Each of these food groups provides some, but not all, of 
the nutrients required. 

® Food in one group can't-replace those in another 



Prudent Diet (Dietary Goals) 

The direct goals (Prudent diet) recommended by the various expert committees of WHO are as below: 

(i) Dietary fat should be limited to approximately 15-30% of total daily int
ake. 

(ii) Saturated fats should contribute no more than 10% of the total energy int
ake. 

(iii) Excessive consumption of refined carboh ydrates should be avoided; some amount of c
arbohydrates 

rich in natural fibres should be taken. 

(iv) Sources rich in energy such as fats & alcohol should be restricted.
 

(v) Salt intake should be reduced to an average of not more than 5g
/ day. 

(vi) Protein should account for approximately 10-15% of the daily intake.
 

(vii) Junk foods such as colas, ketchups and other foods that supply empty calories
 should be reduced.

Under Nutrition 3 

The term under nutrition encompasses stunting (chronic malnutrition), wasting (acute malnutrition) 
and underweight. 

Under nutritioni s not only an important cause of childhood morbidity and mortality, but also leads to 
permanent impairment of physical and possibly of mental growth of those who survives, 

"Protein energy malnutrition"i s a type of malnutrition resulting from deficiency of proteins & calories 
in food over a long period of time. 

Protein energy malnutrition is primarily due to 

(a) An inadequate intake of food (food gap) both in quantity & quality. 

(b) Infections, diarrhoea, respiratory infections, measles and intestinal worms. 

Iti s a vicious circle- infection contributing to malnutrition and malnutrition contribution to infectiocn , 
both acting synergistically.

|Underweight 
e A underweight child has a weight for age Z-score that is atleast two standard deviations (- 2SD) below the 

median in the world health organization (WHO) child growth standards. 
Underweight is a composite measure of stunting and wasting; Underweight can result from either chronic 
or acute malnutrition or both. 

*® Underweight is often used as a basic indicator of the status of a populations health as weight is easy to 
measure. 

Stunting 

|e A stunted child has a height for age Z score that is at least 2 standard deviation (- 2SD) below the median for 
| the WHO child growth standards. 
le Failure to achieve expected height/length as compared to healthy, well nourished children of the same age 

} is a sign of stunting. , | F: 
e Stunting is an indicator of linear growth retardation that results irom failure to sr adequate nutrition 

over a long period or recurrent inadequate nutrition over a long period or recurrent infections. 

® = Itis an indicator of past growth failure. 

i Wasting

ast 2 standard deviation (- 2SD) below the median 

f hild growth standards. | | |

Early Detection of PEM | 

Q The first indicator of PEM is under weight for age; the most practical method to detect this, is to maintain 
growth chart. 

Q Arm circumference yields a relatively reliable estimation of the body's muscle mass, the reduction of which| 
is one of the most striking mechanisms by which the body adjusts to inadequate energy intakes. 

QO ‘Arm circumference cannot be used before the age of one year. | 
e) Arm circumference 

> 13.5 cm - Satisfactory Nutritional status 
12.5-13.5cm - Mild-moderate malnutrition 

| 
<125cm - Severe malnutrition

(iii) Xerophthalmia 

ae 
e It refers to all the ocular manifestations of Vitamin A deficiency in man. 
e Xerophthalmia is most common in children aged 1-3 years. 
e Associated risk factors include ignorance, faulty feeding practices and infections particularly diarrhoea and 

measles which often precipitates xerophthalmia. 

Prevention and Control 

(a) Short term actions - this include administration of large doses of Vitamin A orally, in recommended doses 
to vulnerable groups, on a periodic basis. 

(b) Medium term action - This include fortification of certain foods with Vitamin A; addition of Vitamin A ial 
dalda in India is a typical example. 

(c) Long term action : These measures are aimed at reduction or elimination of factors contributing to ocular! 
disease. 

Example 

e Toconsume dark green leafy vegetable or food rich in Vitamin A 

@ Promotion of breast feeding 

e Ensuring safe and adequate water supply 

@ Maintenance of sanitary latrines to safeguard against diarrhoea. 

e Immunization against infections disease, etc. 

e National programme for prevention of Nutritional blindness focuses On: 

(a) Promoting consumption of vitamin A rich food by pregnant & lactating mother and children under 5 years} 
of age. 

(b) Administration of major doses of Vitamin A upto 5 years; First dose of 1,00,000 [U with measles vaccination) — 
at nine months and subsequent doses of 2,00,000 IU each, every six months upto the age of 5 years. 

(c) For sick children - all children with xerophthalmia to be treated at health facilities; all cases of severe} — 
malnutrition to be given one additional! dose of Vitamin A. : 

(iv) Nutritional Anaemia 

€ It has been defined by WHO as "a condition in which the haemoglobin content of blood is lower than Normal} 
as a result of a deficiency of one or more essential nutrients, regardless of the causes of such deficiency’. | 

e It is found especially among woman of child bearing age, young children and during pregnancy and 
lactation. 

e Iron deficiency can arise either due to inadequate intake or poor bio-availability of dietary iron or due to) — 
excessive losses of iron from the body. ; 

e Poor bio-availability of iron is considered to be a major reason for the widespread iron deficiency. ee 
w Women lose a considerable amount of iron especially during Menstruation. 

¢  Megaloblastic anaemia is not encountered frequently in general population; it is possible that the Widespread a 
iron deficiency (Microcytic anaemia) could mask megaloblastic anaemia.

Lathyrus Sativus 

Figure 

& Also known as khesari dhal, Teora dhal, Lak dhal, etc 

@ Seeds are triangular in shape & grey colour. 

e Toxin present in lathyrus seed is "Beta oxaly! amino alanine". 

® Toxin is water soluble, and this property has been made use of in removing toxin from pulse by soaking it 
in hot water and rejecting the soak water. 

¢ Neurolathyrism is caused by eating the pulse "khesaridhal". 

e Diet containing over 30% of this dhal if taken over a period of 2-6 months will result in Neurolathyrism. 

® The disease effects mainly young men between the age of 15 to 45 years. 

The disease Manifests in following stages 

(a) Latent Stage : Individual is apparently healthy, but when subjected to physical str
ess exhibits ungainly gait. 

e This stage is important from the preventive asp
ect. 

® Withdrawn of pulse from diet, will result in complete re
mission of the disease. 

(b) Nostick stage : Patient walks with short jerky steps without the aid of a stick; a large number of patients are 

found in this stage. 

(c) One stick stage ; Patient walks with a crossed gait with a tendency to walk on toes; 

® Muscular stiffness makes it necessary to use a stic
k to maintain balance. 

(d) Two stick stage : Due to excessive bending of knees and crossed legs, the patient needs two crut
ches for 

support. 

@ The gait is slow and clumpsy. 

» |e) crawler stage : Erect posture becomes impossible as the knee
 joints cannot support the weight of the body. 

e There is atrophy of the thigh and leg muscles

ASSESSMENT OF NUTRITIONAL STATUS 

The main objective of a "comprehensive" nutritional survey is to obtain precise information on the prevalnc 
and geographic distribution of nutritional problems of a given community, and identification of individual 
or population groups "at risk" or in greatest need of assistance. 0 

The nutritional status of a community is the sum of the nutritional status of the individuals who form 2 

community. = 30m of 

The assessment methods include the following % 
(i) Clinical examination 

(ii) Anthropometry 
a 

(iii) Biochemical evaluation 

(iv) Functional assessment 

(v) Assessment of dietary intake 
3 

(vi) Vital and health Statistics 
‘ 

(vii) Ecological studies. 

(i) Clinical Examination 



Clinical signs have the following drawbacks 

(a) Malnutrition cannot be quantified on the basic of clinical signs 
(b) Many deficiencies are unaccompanied by physical signs. 

(c) Lack of specificity and subjective nature of most of the physical signs. 

(i) Anthropometry 

Measurements such as height, weight, skin fold thickness, and arm circumference are valuable indicator of 
nutritional status. 

If anthropometric measurements are recorded over a period of time, they reflect the patterns of growth and 
development. 

)
| iii  Laboratory and Biochemical Assessment

AFLATOXINS 

. This - a special group of fungal mycotoxins produced under conditions of improper Storage, Wyn H V 
Aspergillus flavus and Aspergillus parasiticus. 

Be 
é Aflatoxicosis is characterized by hepatitis, cirrhosis of liver, and enteritis caused by ingestion of infes

f t
o e
o d
d  
 grains. 

e The fungus has been observed to infest improperly stored foods like maize, groundnut, jowar, rice, wheat, 
etc. 

e Most important factors affecting the formation of the toxin are moisture and temperature. 

e Moisture levels above 16% and temperature ranging from 11 to 37°C favour toxin formation. 
° Fungus produces toxin, Aflatoxin B, and G,, which are potent hepatotoxins, in addition to being carcinogenic. 

Prevention and Control 

e Main issue is to confirm proper storage of food items after drying in dry containers. 

¢ Moisture content should be kept as low as 10%. 

¢ Foods contaminated by aflatoxin are not fit for human consumption. 

® It is essential to educate the local population on the health hazards of consuming contaminated food grains.

Prevention of Food Adulteration Act, 1954 

Q The prevention of food adulteration (PFA) act was amended in 1964, 1976 and lately in 1986 to make the act 

more stringent. 

Q@ Aminimum imprisonment of 6 months with a minimum fine of Rs: 1,000 is envisaged under the act for cases 

of proven adulteration. 

9 Cases of adulteration which may render the food injurious to cause death or such harm which may amount 

to grievous hurt, the punishment may go upto life imprisonment & a fine which shall not be 
less than Rs: 

9,000.

The general principles of food hygiene aim to provide: 
* _ To prevent food poisoning and other food borne illnesses. 
* Provide principles and guidance on the application of GHP(Good hygiene practices) applicable throughout 

the food chain to provide food that is safe and suitable for consumption. 
* Provide guidance on the application of HACCP(Hazard Analysis and Critical Control Point) principles, 
* Clarify the relationshi p between GHPs and HACCP.

- Goal - to reduce cholesterol: HDL ratio to < 3.5

Dietary cholesterol consumption < 200 mg/day 

- Reduction of salt intake to <5 gm/day 

- Avoidance of alcohol consumption 

- Increase in complex carbohydrate consumption

Ly. The correct answer is (a) 14 mg 

The additional iron requirement for a pregnant woman in India is 14 
mg 

Iron requirement for a healthy adult female is 21 mg/day and that for pregnant woma
n is 35 mg/day. 

Hence the additional requirement will be 14 mg/day 

Iron requirement during lactation is 21 mg/day 

18. The correct answer is (c) Codex alimentarus 

Codex alimentarus is the principal organ of FAO/WHO standards programme, and formulates food 

standards for international market 

The standards in India are based on standards of codex alimentarus 

19. The correct answer is (c) Whole grains 

Glycemic index is the area under the two hour blood glucose response curve following ingestion of a 
fixed portion of test carbohydrate as a proportion of AUC of standard 

Low GI - Most fruits and vegetables, whole grains, Beans, Lentils and Pasta foods 

Moderate GI - Sucrose, Basmati rice and brown rice 

High GI - Corn flakes, Potatoes, White bread, candies and syrupy foods

Learning Situations : Physical facilities for learning such as institutions, teachers, textbooks etc. 

(iv) Motivation: The powerful motives are encouragement, praise, reward and success. 

(v) Physical health : A physically handicapped person cannot learn 

(vi) Mental health : Worries, anxiety and fears interfere with learning. 

Types of Learning 

There are three types of learning 

(i) | Cognitive learning (knowledge) 

(ii) Affective learning (Attitudes) 

(iii) | Psychomotor learning (Skills) 

Psychologist have proposed a number of theories to find about how learning take place : 

€ Learning by conditioned reflex - It is well known that when dogs see food, they begin to salivate (conditioned reflex). 

e Trial & error - This method is very slow, laborious & primitive. 

« Learning by observation & limitation - A child copies or imitates gestures, facial expressions & movements 
such as walking. 

€ Learning by doing - in this type of learning there is coordination of muscular responses with sensory 

impulses. 

e Learning by remembering - We also learn by memorizing. 

° Learning by insight - When we are faced with a problem we solve it by insight or mental exploration. 

6 Demonstration 

€ Demonstration: A procedure is carried out step by step slowly and accurately before audience; the 
demonstrator ascertaining that the audience understand how to perform it. 

« Field Experience: It involve a series of activities for diagnosing problems, planning procedures to solve them

DEFENCE MECHANISM 

When an individual is faced with problems, difficulties or failures, he employs certain ways or devices to 
achieve health, happiness or success; these are called defence mechanisms; it includes 
(i) | Rationalization : Instead of accepting failure & correcting himself, the individual tries to make excuses and 

justifies his behaviour. 

(ii) | Projection : Sometimes the individual blames other for his mistakes or failures. 
(iii) | Compensation : Many people make use of compensation to enhance their self esteem & prestige. 
(iv) Escape mechanisms : Some students pretend illness & do not appear for examinations; this is escape 

mechanisms. 

(v) Displacement : It is trying to escape from one situation & fixing blame on another situation 
(vi) Regression : Some people resort to childhood practices ( Eg: weeping when something goes

INTELLIGENT QUOTIENT 
It is a score derived from one of several different standardized tests attempting to measure intelligence. 
It is obtained by dividing the mental age by chronological age and multiplying by 100.

Types of Families 4 
(i) | Nuclear family: It consists of the married couple & their children while they are still regarded as depende 1s 

e In Nuclear family, the husband usually plays a dominant role in the household. | 

(ii) Joint Family: It consist of no.of married couples and their children who lives together in the same household, | 

e All males are related by blood while females are wives, daughters, sisters & widows. |

| Mother & Child Health 

The various customs in the field of MCH have been classified as good, bad, unimportant & uncertain. 

Good - Breast feeding, oil bath, massage & exposure to sun. 

Bad - Branding of the skin, administration of opium & drastic purgatives are all bad customs. 

Un-important - punching the ear and nose, application of oil or a paste of turmeric on the anterior 
fontanelle. 

q e Uncertain - Practice of applying kajal or black soot mixed with oil to the eye lids.

DOCTOR PATIENT RELATIONSHIP 
e The doctor must know how to communicate with his patient. 
° A successful doctor is one who knows how well to communicate with his patient. 

In this regard, three levels of communications have been describe: 
| 
(i) _ Communication on an Emotional Plane 

The doctor must give a sympathetic ear to the complaints made by the patient & his relatives. 
e The reason why folk medicine is successful is because the patient & his relatives feel they can talk more 

freely to a folk medical practitioner than with the modern physician. 
(ij) _ Communication on a Cultural Plane 

-@ = The doctor should be aware of the general concepts of culture & social organization of the community|  | | 
with which he is dealing. 

e To be successful, the modern doctor should couch his scientific advice in terms which fit an already | 
existing cultural pattern. 

e A mere statement to a patient that the medicine is 'hot' and will help to cure a 'cold' disease may make | 
for increased confidence. 

(iii) Communication on an Intellectual Plane 

e There isa wide gap between the intellectual level of the practitioners of modern medicine & the illiterate 
masses. 

e  Inother words, there is an enormous "social distance" between the two groups. 

e Asuccessful doctor is one who reduces this distance & is able to communicate with his patient freely & 

wins his confidence. 

e A most important component of doctor patient communication is humour. 

CONSUMER PROTECTION ACT 

- The consumer protection Act 1986 provided consumers a forum for speedy redressal of their gr
ievances 

against medical services. 

° Over the centuries the medical profession has been accorded respect by the socie
ty; since last decade or so, 

increasing commercialization of the profession h
as eroded this faith. 

- COPRA is a piece of comprehensive legislation & recognizes six right
s of the consumer namely : 

e Right to safety 

e Right to be informed 

e Right to choose 

e Right to be heard 

e Right to seek redressal 

e Right to consumer education
 

® If a patient or the relations of a patient feel that the suffering or 
death of a patient is because of either 

tec’ by the concerned doctor or the health facility, they c
an complain to the medical council of India 

or to the consumer court. 

® The consumer court can only give compensation, t
he monetary limits of the compensation that can be

 

granted by the consumer court 
are as follows; 

(i) District consumer court - upto Rs. 20
 lacs 

(ii) State commission - Rs. 20 lacs to Rs.
 1 crore 

(iii) National commission - above Rs. 1 cro
re 



Rights of the patient 

(i) | Right to information on health care services available 
to them, diagnosis & treatment. 

(ii) Right to have information about professionals invo
lved in the patient care. 

(iii) Right to safety from errors & malpractice 

(iv) Right to confidentiality & privacy 

(v) Right to have prompt treatment in an emergency. 

(vi) Right to informed consent. 

(vii) Right to get copies of medical records. 

(viii) Right to complain & have compensation within rea
sonably short time.

DRUG ADDICTION 

It is defined as a state of periodic or chronic intoxication detrimental to the individual and soc
iety produce 

by the repeated intake of habit forming drugs. 

"Drug culture" is fast making in roads into the lives of young people from all ways of life. ‘ 

Reasons given for drug dependence include the following; 9 

(i) Curiosity & Natural tendency to experiment with drugs. 

(ii) Disturbed home environment. 

(iii) An escape phenomenon from tensions & frustration in life. 

(iv) Impact of disco culture, mobile, TV, internet etc. 

To call a person a drug addict, the following criteria must be satisfied : 

(i) Psychological dependence - (Compulsion to take drugs & obtain it by any means) 
(ii) Physical dependence - (When drug is withdrawn, the patient shows "withdrawal symptoms’). 
(iii) Development of tolerance (Tendency to increase the dose). 
Management 

(i) Identification of drug addicts & their motivation for drug detoxification 
(ii) Detoxification (require hospitalization) : 
(ili) Post detoxification counselling & follow up 
(iv) Rehabilitation

Social Insurance - Contributory benefit extended to individuals as
 matter of right

t recognized 

conditions like Poverty, old age, disability and unemployment 

The correct answer is (b) <2100 KCal per day

WATER cM 3.1 

Safe and Wholesome Water 

e It is defined as water that is : 

+ Free from pathogenic agents 
+ Free from harmful chemical substances 

+ Pleasant to taste (free from colour and odour) 

+ Usable for domestic purposes. 

© Water is said to be polluted or contaminated if it does not fulfill above criteria. 

Sources of Water 

e There are three main sources of water

Rain 

° It is the prime source of all water. 

e It is the purest form of water in nature. 

€ Chemically, it is very soft water, contain traces (
0.000%5) of solids. 

° Very few places in the world like Gibraltar depend upon rain as a source
 of water supply. 

Surface Water 

° impounding reservoir : These are attificial lakes, in which large quantity of surface water is stored. 

° Dams built across rivers and mountain streams also provide large reserves of s
urface water. 

° Impounding reservoirs usually furnish a fairly good qualit
y of water. 

° One disadvantage of storing water for long period in reservoir is the growth of algae and other microscopic 

organism. 

Rivers 

- Many rivers furnish a dependable supply of water. 

Chief drawback of river water is that it is always gr ossly polluted and is quite unfit for drinking without 

treatment. The bacteriabcountincluding the human intestinal organisms may be very high.

Sea Water 
© Though this source is plentiful, it has many great limitation.

e Desalting and demineralization process involves heavy expenditure.

Ground Water 
e Rain water percolating into ground constitute ground water. 

f . Water used by humans comes mainly from land. 
| . The usual ground water sources are well and springs. 

« Advantages 

» It is likely to be free from pathogenic agents. 
>» It usually requires no treatment. 
» The supply is likely to be certain even during dry season. 
> It is less subject to contamination than surface water. 

« Disadvantages 

» Itis high in mineral content. 
>» Itrequire pumping or some arrangement to lift the water.

PURIFICATION OF WATER CM 3.2 

e Purification of water is of great importance in community medicine; it may be considered under two heading: 

(i) Purification of water on a large scale. 

(ii) Purification of water on a small scale.

& The components of a typical water purification system comprise one or more of the following measures: 

(i) Storage 

(ii) Filtration 

(iii) Disinfection

CHLORINATION 

€ Chlorination is one of the greatest advances in water purification. 

e Chlorine killsp athogenic bacteria, but has no effect on spores and certain viruses, except in high doses. 

7 Disinfection action of chlorine in water is due to: 

+ Hypochlorous acid (HOCL) ? main role in disinfection. 

+ Hypochlorite ions (OCI) : Minor role in disinfection 

‘ Chlorine acts best as a disinfection when the pH of water is around 7; when pH value exceeds 8.5 it is 

unreliable as a disinfectant because 90% of hypochlorous acid gets ionized to hypochlorite ions

Tests for Chlorination of Water 

e  Orthotolidine Test (OT) 

+ Measures the level of free residual chlorine and true & combined chlorine together. 

+ The test is carried out by adding 0.1 ml of the reagent to 1 ml of
 water; the yellow colour produced is 

matched against suitable standards or colour discs. 

¢ Orthotolidine Arsenite (OTA) Test : 

+ Measure the level of free chlorine and combined chlorine sep
arately. 

+ Detects both free and combined chlorine separately. 

+  Notaffected by interfering substances (nitrites, ir
on, manganese).

Disinfection of Wells 

Wells are the main source of water supply in the rural areas. i} cs 
The most effective and cheapest method of disinfecting wells is by bleaching powder. ; f 
Steps in well disinfection 

ie 
(i) Find the volume of water in a well 

> 

Volume (litre) = 3.147x d°*x h «1000 

h = depth of water column » 

d = diameter of well 

(ii) Find the amount of bleaching powder required for disinfection: 

Estimate the chlorine demand of the well water by "Horrocks Apparatus" and calculate the amount of | 
bleaching powder required to disinfect the well. ili 

(iii) Dissolve Bleaching Powder in Water 

Bleaching powder required for disinfecting well is placed in a bucket and made into a thin paste; more} ; 
water is added till the bucket is nearly three fourths full. 4 

The content is stirred well and allowed to sediment for 5-10 minutes. 

The supernatant solution, whichi s hiiclive solution, is transferred to another bucket and the chalk or} 
limei s discarded. 

(iv) Delivery of Chlorine Solution into the Well 

The bucket containing the chlorine solutioni s lowered some distance below the water surface, and the} 
well water is agitated by moving the bucket violently both vertically and laterally. 

Contact Period 

e Acontact period of one hour is allowed before the water is drawn for use. 

Orthotolidine Arsenite Test 

If free residual chlorine leveli s less than 0.5 mg/litre he h ion procedure should be repeatec 
before any water is drawn.

During emergency, it is desirable to ensure a constant dosage of chlorine to wel
l water. 

‘ This method uses two cylindrical pots, one placed inside the other. 

A hole 1 cm in diameter is made in each pot; the hole is in upper portion nea
r the rim in the inner pot and 4 

cm above the bottom in the outer pot. 

The inner pot is filled with a mixture of 1 kg of bleaching powder and 2 kg of coarse sa
nd, slightly moistened 

with water. 

The inner pot is introduced into the outer one, and the mouth of the latter closed with polyethyle
ne foil. 

The double pot is lowered into the well by means of a rope attached to the well
 kerb. 

° The pot should be immersed atleast 1m, below the water level to prevent damage by buckets used for 

drawing water. 

e The device works satisfactorily for 2-3 weeks in small household
 wells. 

WATER QUALITY 

The guidelines for drinking water quality recommended by WHO relate to following var
iable : 

(i) Acceptability aspects 

(ii) Microbiological aspect 

(iii) Chemical aspect 

(iv) Radiological aspect 

(i) Acceptability Aspect 

(a) Physical Parameters 

Turbidity : It is caused by particulate matter; water with turbid; ty of more than 4 ne phelometric turbidity unit (NTU) is usually noticeable to the naked eye. 

e Colour: Drinking water should be free from colour;/  tthhee  guiodujedelli; ne value is y 
me Pto 15 true colour (TCV uni ) ts 

|4 63| 



=_ 

: | 
Taste and Odour : An unusual taste or odour might be an indication of the presence of potentially 
harmful substances; No health based guidelines value is proposed for taste & odour. 

¢ Temperature : Cool water is more palatable; No guidelines value is recommended since its control 
is usually impracticable. 

(b) Inorganic Constituents 

¢ Chlorides - Standard prescribed for chloride is 200 mg/litre; maximum permissible level is 
600 mg/ litre 

e Hardness - The taste threshold for the calcium ion is in the range of 100-300 mg/ litre. 

e Ammonia - Nitrate < 45 mg/litre; Nitrites <3 mg/litre. 

e pH- Anacceptable pH drinking water is between 6.5 and 8.5. 

e Total dissolved solids - the palatability of water with a TDS level of less than 60 mg/litre is generally 
considered to be good. 

(ii) Microbiological Aspect oy 

e Bacteriological Indicators yell 

—S, Coliform Organisms ~~) y 

e Primary & most reliable bacterial indicator for water quality. oe roa 

e E.coil is most important coliform indicator. ; 

e Fecal streptococci indicate recent contamination of water. pil iad r 

e Clostridium perfringes indicate remote contamination of water. 

7 Virological Aspects 

e Drinking water should be free from any viruses infectious for man. 

e Disinfection with 0.5 mg/] of free chlorine residual after contact period of at least 30 minutes at a pH of 
8.0 is sufficient to inactivate virus. 

— 

Biological Aspect | 
e Drinking water should not contain any pathogenic intestinal protozoa; helminths'etc. 

(iii) Chemical Aspects | 

e The chemical selected for the development of guidelines value include those considered potentially | 
hazardous to human health, those detected relatively frequently in drinking water and those detected 
in relatively high concentration. 

Antimony 0.02 mg/litre 

| Arsenic 0.01 mg/litre 

| Chromium 0.05 mg/litre 

Fluoride 1.5 mg/litre 

| Copper | 2 mg/litre ) 

| Manganese 0.4 mg/litre | 

Nitrate 3 mg/ litre of |

HARDNESS OF WATER 
° Hardness may be defined as the Soap destroying power of water. 
e The hardness in w ater is c aused mainly by four dissolved compounds; these are calcium bicarbonate, 

magne siu: m bi ean em calci ium sulphate and magnesium sulphate. 

Temporary hardness (carbonate hardness) Calcium and magnesium salts of Bicarbonates 
Permanent hardness (Non carbonate hard- Calcium and magnesium salts of sulfates, chlorides & 
ness) Nitrates 

. Hardness in water is expressed in terms of "milli equivalent per litre (mEq/L) 
7 One mEq/L of hardness = 50 mg CaCO, (50 ppm) per litre of water. 

C lassification of hardness in water 

or 4 
ry ig! ol dt 

Soft water <1 (< 50 mg/ litre) 

Moderately hard | 1-3 (50-150 mg/litre) 

Hard water 3-6 (150-300 mg/litre) 

Very hard water | > 6 (> 300 mg/litre) 

° Drinking water should be moderately hard. 

° Softening of water is recommended when the hardness exceeds 3 mEq/
litre (150 mg/litre) 

° The methods of removal of hardness are
 briefly stated as below : 

Temporary hardness Boiling 

Addition of lime 

Addition of sodium carbonate 

Permutit process 

Permanent hardness ~
 Addition of sodium carbo

nate» 4).'" 

Base exchange process 

orary hardness by expelling carbon dioxide, and precipitating
 the insoluble

calcium carbonate.

(ii) Addition of Lime

(iii) Addition of Sodium Carbonate 

¢ Sodium carbonate (Soda ash) removes both temporary & permanent hardness. 

¢ Na,CO,+Ca(HCO,),— 2N aHCO, + CaCO, 

e CaSO, + Na,CO, > CaCO, + NaSO, 

(iv) Base Exchange Process 

¢ Sodium permutit is a complex compound of sodium, aluminium & silica (Na,Al,Si,OH,O) 

e When hard water is passed through the permutit the calcium and magnesium ions are entirely removed| 
by base exchange & the sodium permutit is finally converted into calcium & magnesium permutit. 

e  Permutit process removes both temporary & permanent hardness. 

HORROCK'S APPARATUS 

Horrock's Apparatus is used to find out the dose of bleaching powder required for disinfection of water 
(chlorine demand of water). 

Content : c — 

6 White cups (200 ml capacity each) 

1 black cup with a circular mark on inside 

2 metal spoons 

7 glass stirring rods 

1 pipette, 2 droppers 

Starch iodide indicator solution 

Procedure 

Take one level spoonful (2g) of bleaching powder in the black cup; add water and make up the volume 
upto circular mark with vigorous stirring. 

1 

Allow to settle; this is stock solution 

J 
Fill the 6 white cups with water to be tested, upto about a cm below the brim 

+ 
With pipette add 1 drop of stock solution to 1* cup, 2 drops of stock solution to 2™4 cup, 3 drop to 3% cup & 

SO on. 

F 
Stir the water in each cup using a separate rod. 

L 
Wait for half an hour for action of chlorine 

+ 
Add 3 drops of starch iodide indicator to each white cup and stir again 

1 
Note the first cup which shows distinct blue colour ; 

Development of blue colour indicates the presence of free residual chlorine.

Air Pollutants 

€ Pollutants may be in the forms of solids, liquids (vapour) or gases. 

® Some of the important air pollutants 

(a) Carbon Monoxide 

e = It is one of the most common & widely distributed air pollutants. 

e It is a product of incomplete combustion of carbon containing materials such as in automobiles,| 
industrial process, heating facilities & incinerators. 

({b) Sulphur Dioxide 

e Sulphur exists in several forms such as SO,, H,S, H,SO,, and sulphate salts. 

¢ Sulphur dioxide is a colourless gas with sharp odour results from combustion of sulphur containing fossil 
fuel. 

« When SO, combines with water, it forms sulphuric acid; this is the main component of acid rain which 
is a cause of deforestation. 

(c) Lead 

e People can become expo:tso eledad through occupational and environmental sources. 
e _ Lead exposure occur through‘inhalation of lead particles, ingection of lead contaminated dust and use 

of some traditional cosmetics & medicines. 

e Lead affect's brain developmentiresulting in reduced intelligence quotient (IQ); the neurological &| : 
behavioural effect of lead are believed to be irreversible. 

{d) Hydrocarbons 

e Hydrocarbons exert their pollutant action by taking part in the chemical reaction that causeli=il  
photochemical smog. 

fe) Ozone 
e Ozone at ground level is one of the major constituents of photochemical smog. 

« It can cause breathing problems, trigger asthma, reduce lung function and.cause lung disease.

le cag ir pollution can affect by two ways: 

e The health effects of air pollution are both immediate and delayed. 

(* aay | e Immediate effects are borne by the respiratory system, the resulting state is acute bronchitis. 

i e The delayed effects most commonly linked with air pollution are chronic bronchitis, lung cancer, | | 

bronchial asthma, emphysema, & respiratory allergies. 7 fees 

| Example: Lead — impaired neuropsychological development in children 

Hydrocarbons —> lung cancer : 

{b) Social and Economic Aspects 

e These comprise destruction of plant and animal life; corrosion of metals; damage to buildings; cost of 
cleaning & maintenance & repairs and aesthetic nuisance. 

Prevention and Control of Air Pollution 

. The WHO has recommended the following procedures for the prevention & control of air pollution: 

+ Containment (prevention of escape of toxic substances into ambient air); it can be achieved by a variety 

of engineering methods such as enclosure, ventilation, & air cleaning. 4 

+ Replacement / replacing a technological process causing air pollution, by a new process that does not 

Example : Use of electricity, solar power generation, natural gas etc in place of coal. 

+ Dilution : Some air pollutants are readily removed by vegetations. Establishment of green belts between| 
industrial & residential area is an attempt at dilution. 

+ Legislation : Legislation covers such matters as height of chemicals, power to local authorities to carry 
out investigations, research & education concerning air pollution, creation of smokeless zones & 
enforcement of standard for ambient air quality. 

Example : "The Air (prevention & control of pollution) Act" in 1981. 

+ International Action : The WHO has established an international network of laboratories for the 

monitoring & study of air pollution; these centres will issue warnings of air pollution where & when 
necessary.

Types of Radiation 

(a) _ lonizing radiation : Radiations which has the ability to penetrate into tissues & deposit its energy within them. 

+ lonizing radiations may be divided into two main groups : Electromagnetic radiations (X-rays, gamma 

rays) corpuscular radiations (Alpha particle, beta particle & proton). 

Non-ionizing radiations : It refers to several forms of electromagnetic radiations of wavelengths longer than (b) 
those of ionizing radiation. 

Example : UV radiation, visible light, infrared radiation, microwave radiation etc.

High altitudes 

+ When man is exposed to low pressures, the physiological effects are; 

(i) Increase in respiration 

(ii) Increase in the concentration of haemoglobin 

(iii) Increase in cardiac output

Effects of Heat Stress 

The important effects of heat stress 
(i) Heat Stroke 

It is attributed to failure of the heat regulating mechanisms. 
It is characterized by very high body temperature which many rise to 110°F (43.3°C) and profound 
disturbances including delirium, convulsion and partial or complete loss of consciousness. 

e The outcome is often fatal, even when patient are brought quickly to medical attention. 
e The treatment consists of rapidly cooling the body in ice water both till the rectal temperature falls 

below 102°F (38.9°C),. 

(ii) Heat Hyperpyrexia 

e It is arbitrarily defined as a temperature above 106°F. 

e It is attributed to impaired functioning of the heat regulating mechanism but without characteristic 
features of heat stroke. 

(iii) Heat Exhaustion 

e It is caused primarily by the imbalance or inadequate replacement of water and salt lost in perspiration 
due to thermal stress. 

¢ The symptoms primarily dizziness, weakness and fatigue, are those of circulatory distress. 

e Treatment is directed towards normalizing fluid and electrolyte balance. 

(iv) Heat Cramps 

e These are painful and spasmodic contractions of the skeletal muscles. 

e Heat cramps occur in persons who are doing heavy muscular work in high temperature and humidity. 

(v) Heat Syncope 

@ This is a common ill effect of heat 

e The person standing in the sun becomes pale, his blood pressure falls and he collapses suddenly. 

e There is practically no rise in temperature. 

Preventive Measures 

(i) Replacement of Water 

e Person working under conditions of high temperature & humidity should be encouraged to drink cool 
water. 

¢ Aman doing hard work in the sun requires about one litre of water per hour. 

e Salt losses through sweat in very small quantity, therefore there is no need to add salt to water. | |

(v) Work Environment 

¢ The temperature and humidity in the work environment may be controlled by proper ventilation & air : 
condition. 

GLOBAL WARMING 

€ Global warming increases the earth's average surface temperature due to effect of green house gases in the | a 
atmosphere produced due to carbon dioxide emission by burning fossil fuels or from deforestation, which ia 
traps the heat that would otherwise escape from earth. | 

« It is generally conceded that the main effect of this include an increase of about 3°C in the average global} ; 
surface temperature by the year 2030, a rise in the sea level of 0.1-0.3 meters by 2050, an increase in occurence , 
of extreme climatic events such as cyclones, heatwaves and droughts. 

e Examples of greenhouse gases : Water vapour, Carbondioxide, methanal, Nitrous oxide, Hydro-| 
fluorocarbons, Nitrogen trifluoride etc. 

° Effects of Global Warming Le 

(i) Geometeorological effects (Heat stress, Rising sea levels, Weather disasters) ; 

(ii) Biological effects (Many species will get extinct) 

(iii) Socioeconomic effect (Low yield from crops, low gross domestic product) 

(iv) Health impacts / effects (Asthma, respiratory allergies, cardiovascular disease, Heat related morbidity 
& mortality etc.)

Septic Tank 

The septic tank is water tight masonry tank into whi
ch household sewage is admitted for treatment. 

It is a satisfactory means of disposing excreta and liquid w
astes from individual dwellings, small groups of 

houses and institutions which have adequate water supplies but d
o not have access to a public sewerage 

system. 

Design Features 

(i) Capacity : It depends upon the number of users; minimum capacity of a
 septic tank should be at least 500 

gallons. 

(ii) Length : Length is usually twice the breadth 

(iii) Depth: itis 1.5-2m 

(iv) Liquid Depth : Recommended liquid depth is only 1.2 m. 

(v) Air Space : Minimum air space is 30 cms. 

(vi) Bottom : The bottom is sloping towards the inlet end. 

(vii) Inlet & Outlet (which are submerged) 

(viii) Cover : Septic tank is covered by a concrete slab of suitable thickness and provided with a manh
ole. 

(ix) Retention period : 24 hours.

SEWAGE 
Sewage is waste water from a community, containing solid & liquid excreta, derived from houses, street and 

yard washing, factories & industries. 

Sullage is waste water which does not contain human excreta, 

Example : Waste water from kitchens & bathrooms. 

The amount of sewage that flows in the sewers depends upon habits of the people and time of day. 

The average amount of sewage which flows through the sewerage system in 24 hours is called the "dry 

weather flow". 

Sewage contains 99.9% of water; the solids which comprise barely 0.1% 
are partly organic & partly inorganic. 

The offensive nature of the sewage is mainly due to the organic nature which it contains
. 

The strength of sewage 1s expressed in terms of
 : 

(i) Biological Oxygen Demand (BOD) : It is defined as amount of oxygen absorbed by a sample of sewage 

during a specified period (5 days), at a specified temperature (generally 20°C) for aerobic destruction 

or use of organic matter by living organi
sm. 

> Strong sewage has BOD > 300 g/litre 

> Weak sewage has BOD < 100 g/litre 

(ii) Chemical Oxygen Demand (COD) : Measures oxygen equivalent of that portion of organic matter in a 
sample which is susceptible to oxidation by a strong chemical oxidizer; potassium dichromate is best 

for COD estimation. 

(iii) Suspended Solids : Amount of suspended solids in domestic sewage varies from 100-500 mg/ litre. 

> Strong sewage has suspended solids amount > 500 mg/ litre 

> Weak sewage has suspended solids amount < 100 mg/ litre 
ee| 



_ ——— 

Modern Sewage Treatment an 

° The treatment of sewage may be divided into two stages, primary treatment and 
secondary treatment. 

e In primary treatment, the solids are separated from the sewage pat tly by screening and partly yy | 
sedimentation and subjected to anaerobic digestion. 

e In secondary treatment the effluent is subjected to aerobic oxidation. 

Primary Treatment 

(i) Screening 

+ The screen consists of vertical or inclined steel bars usually set 5 cm apart. 

+ Metal screen intercepts large floating objects such as pieces of wood, rags, garbage & dead animals and 
| 

prevent clogging of treatment plant. 

(ii) | Grit Chamber 

+ The function of the grit chamber is to allow the settlement of heavier solids such as sand & gravel, while} 

permitting the organic matter to pass through. | 

| + Grit chamber is a long narrow chamber of approximately 10-20 m in length. 

+ Itisso designed as to maintain a constant velocity of about 1 foot per second with a detention period of 
30 seconds to 1 minute. 

(iii) Primary Sedimentation 

+ Itis avery large rectangular tank, holding from 1/4 - 1/3 the dry weather flow. 

+ Sewage flows at a velocity of 1-2 feet per minute across the tank. 

+ Sewage spends about 6-8 hours in the tank; during this a very considerable amount of purification takes 
place mainly through sedimentation of suspended matter. | 

+ Nearly 50-70% of solids settle down under the influence of gravity. 

Secondary Treatment 

e The effluent from the primary sedimentation tank is subjected to further treatment, aerobic oxidation by one 
of the following methods : 

(i) Trickling filter method 

| (ii) Activated sludge process 

(i) Trickling Filter Method 

+ The trickling filter or percolating filter is a bed 

of crushed stones or cinker 1 to 2 m deep & 
2-30 m in diameter, depending upon the size of the 

population. 

+ The effluent from the primary sedimentation tank 
is sprinkled uniform on the surface of the bed by a 

revolving device. 

+ Over the surface and down through the filter, a ver
. y complex biological owth consisting of alga 4 

fungi, protozoa & bacteria of many kinds occur; this is known as the "z sie 
g 8 i 

leal layer". 

| + Aseffluent percolates through the filter bed, it gets oxidized by the bacterial flora in the zoogleal layet} 
+ The trickling filters are very efficient in purifying sewa ge. 

|

+ The oxidized sewage is now led into the 
secondary sedimentation ake gecee 

to | 

adie 



(il) Activated Sludge Process —s | : 
+ Tahe e f drei from the primary sedimentation tank [| ~ | : } er 

xed with Sludge drawn from the final settling ume 
tank (also known as activated sludge; this sludge is 
a rich culture of aerobic bacteria). 
The proportion of activated Sludge to the incoming 
effluent is of the order of 20-30%. 
The mixture is subjected to aeration in the aeration 
chamber for about 6-8 hours. 

+ The aeration is accomplished either by mechanical 
agitat Figure 

ion or by forcing compressed air continuously 
from the bottom of aeration tank (known as diffuse 
aeration; better method of aeration). 

+ During the process of aeration, the organic matter of the sewage gets oxidized with the help of aerobic 
bacteria in the activated sludge. 

+ Activated sludge plants occupy less space, require skilled operation. 
+ One acre of activated sludge plant does the work of 10 acres of percolating filter. 

Secondary Sedimentation 

The oxidized sewage is led into the secondary sedimentation tank where it is detained for 2-3 hours. 

The sludge that collects in the secondary sedimentation tank is called ‘aerated sludge' or activated sludge 

because it is fully aerated. 

It differs from the sludge in the primary sedimentation tank in that it is practically inoffensive and is rich in 

bacteria, nitrogen & phosphates. 

It is a valuable manure, if dehydrated. 

Part of activated sludge is pumped back into the aeration tank and rest is pumped into the sludge digestion 

tanks for treatment & disposal. 

Sludge Digestion 

+ One million gallons of sewage produces 15-20 tons of sludge. 

+ Methods of sludge disposal 

(a) Digestion 

e If sludge is incubated under favourable conditions of temperature & pH,
 it undergoes anaerobic auto 

digestion. 

© Volume of sludge is also considerably red
uced. 

© _ It takes 3-4 weeks or longer for complet
e sludge digestion. 

e The residue is inoffensive, sticky and tarry mud which will dry readily and
 form an excellent manure. 

(b) Sea Disposal 

© Seacoast town & cities can dispose of sludge by pumpin
g it into the sea. 

(c) Land 

e Sludge can be disposed of by composting with town 
refuse. 

Disposal of Effluent 

(a) Disposal by Dilution 

e Disposal into water courses such as river & streams is called disposal by dilution. 

© Theeffluentis diluted in the body of water & impurities are oxidized by the dissolved oxygen in water.

Designing of Oxidation Pond e | 

© A cheap method of sewage treatment is the oxidation pond; also known as waste stabilization pond, redox 
pond, sewage lagoons, etc. le 

7 The term waste include both sewage & industrial waste. de 

‘ The oxidation pond is an open, shallow pool 1 to 1-5 m deep with an inlet & outlet. lf 
« To qualify as oxidation pond, there must be presence of : ke 

fy Algae ; 

\f ii) Certain type of bacteria which feed on decaying organic matter. i 

« The organic matter contained in the sewage is oxidized by bacteria to simple chemical compounds such as i 
carbon dioxide, ammonia & water. 1 | Ways, WYO 

© The algae, with the help of sunlight utilize the carbon dioxide, water & inorganic minerals for their co g 
e Thus, there is a mutually beneficial biological balance between the algae and bacteria in oxidation ponds. f 

* Oxygen that is neeford oxiedatdion  is derived to a small extent from the atmosphere but mostly from the 
algae which liberates oxygen under the influence of sunlight. ve 

* Oxygen ponds are predominantly aerobic during sunshine hours as well as some hours of the night. 
e  Inremaining hours of night, the bottom layers are generally anaerobic. ii 
° Thus the sewage purification in oxidation pond is brought about by a combination of aerobi 

types of bacteria. _

Principles of Arthropod Control 

The general principles of arthropod control are 

(i) | Environmental Control 

e _ Best approach to control of arthropods, because 
results are likely to be permanent. 

© Example : Elimination of breeding places, filling & drainage 
operation etc. 

(ii) | Chemical Control 

e No longer effective if used alone. 

e Resistance has appeared in about 100 species of arthropods of public health importance. | 

© Toavoid undue environmental pollution, it is now considered essential to replace gradually the highly) 

persistent compounds such as DDT with compounds which are readily "biodegradable" and less toxic) 

to man and animals such, as methoxychlor, abate & dursban. 

(iii) Biological Control 

e Minimizes environmental pollution. 

e Example 

>  Larvivorous fishes (Gambusia, lebister) 

> Fungi (Coelomomyces) 

> Bacteria (Bacillus thuringiensis H14) 

» Predator mosquito (Toxorhynchitis splendens) 

(iv) Genetic Control 

e Techniques such as sterile male techniques, cytoplasmic incompatibility, chromosomal translocation 

have been found to be effective in small field trials. 

(v) Newer Method 

¢ — Example : Insect growth regulators, chemosterilants, pheromones (sex attractants etc).

Anti-Larval Methods

Chemical Control , 4 

+ Mineral Oil : Oldest know mosquito control measure. 

> Most widely used oils are diesel oil, fuel oil, kerosene and various fractions of crude oils 

> Oil kills larvae and pupae within a short time after application. 

> Disadvantage : It renders water unfit for drinking; it kills fish. 

+ Paris Green: Paris green or copper acetoarsenite is a emerald green, microcrystalline powder pra 
insolublei n water & is a stomach poison; 

> Kills mainly the Anopheles larvae because they are surface feeders 
' >» Recommended dose is 1 kg of actual paris green per hectare of water surface

 does not harm fish, man or do
mestic animals.

+ Synthetic insecticides:  Fenthion, chlorpyrifos and Abate are the most effective larvicides.

Transmission of Disease 

e Flies transmit disease in the following ways : 

(i) Mechanical Transmission 

+ They transport microorganisms on their feet and hairy legs. 

+ Pathogenic organisms, ova and cysts have been recovered from the bodies of the common housefly, _ 

+ Hbouseflies are therefore called "porters of infection". 

(ii) ‘Vomit Drop - the regurgitated stomach content or vomit drop is a rich bacterial culture. 

(iii) | Defecation - The excrement of housefly has been found to contain numerous micro organisms as well as 

cysts and ova of intestinal parasites. 

Fly Control Measures 

(i) Environmental Control 

* The best way to control houseflies is to eliminate their breeding places and to bring about an over all 

improvement in the environmental sanitation. 

+ Storing garbage, kitchen wastes and other refuse in bins with tight lids. 

+ Efficient collection, removal and disposal of refuse. 

+ Provision of sanitary latrines. 

+ Stepping up general sanitation. 

(ii) Insecticidal Control 

e Residual Chlorine - Susceptible flies may be killed by DDT, mathoxychlor, lindane, chlordane etc. 

e _ Baits - Baits may be solid or liquid. 

+ Poisoned baits containing 1 or 2% diazinon, malathion, dichlorvos, ronnel and dimethoate have Be 
been tried with success. rf

“leas are aa | bilaterally compressed, wingless insects with a hard chitinous exoskeleton and covered 

| packs vardly directed strong bristles. 
—- 

1ey are blood sucking ectoparasites of mammals & birds. 
af z _ The rat fleas are of greatest importance because they are vectors of plague and typhus. 
py Fleas of importance in public health are 

Rat flea (oriental) —» — Xenopsylla cheopis, Xenopsylla astia, Xenopsylla braziliensis 
Human flea —  Pulex irritans 1 
Dog & Cat flea = -» = Ctenocephalus canis, Ctenocephalus felis | | 
Sand flea —  Tunga penetrans i 

Modes of Transmission 
(i) Biting 

® The chief method of transmission, in the case of plague, is by the bite of hungry 'blocked' fleas 
Some fleas which ingest plague bacilli become blocked due to the multiplication of plague bacilli in | 
their proventriculus or stomach; fleas affected in this way are called 'blocked fleas’. 

(ii) | Mechanical Transmission 

e Takes place from the proboscis of the fleas, which had recently fed on an infected rodent. | | : 
(iii) Faeces 

| 
& Faecal drop of infected flea may contain numerous bacilli. 

Control of Flea 
(i) Insecticidal Control ihe 

e Cheapest and most widely used formulation has been 10% DDT dust. 
e — Inareas where fleas have developed resistance to DDT, carbaryl or diazinon or malathion are used. J 

Patch dusting with insecticides has also been found to be an effective method of controlling flea. 
Insecticidal dust should be blown into the rodent's burrow with the help of dust blowers. 

(ii) Repellents 

e — Diethyltolumide is an efficient flea repellent 
e Benzyl benzoate is also a good flea repellent. 

(iii) | Rodent Control 

e Flea control should be followed by rodent control. 
Flea Indices 

(i) | General flea index = It is average number of fleas of all species per rodent. 
(ii) | Specific flea index = Average number of flea of each species, found per rodent. 
(iii) Percentage incidence of flea species = Percentage of fleas of each species, found per rodent. 
(iv) Rodent infestation rate = Percentage of rodents infested with the various flea species. 
Cyclops 

« Cyclops or water flea is a crustacean present in most collection of fresh water. 
« It is a tiny arthropod (not more than 1mm), has pear shaped semi-transparent body, a forked tail, 2 pairs of 

antennae, 5 pairs of legs and a small pigmented eye. ; 
° Average life of a cyclops is about 3 months. 
« Cyclops is the intermediate host of Guinea worm disease, | 
e Cyclops is one of the intermediate host of fish tape worm, Diphyllobothrium latum.

Anti-rodents Measures 

(i) Sanitation measures : It comprise 

e Proper storage, collection and disposal of garbage. 

e Proper storage of food stuff. 

e Construction of rat proof buildings, godowns and warehouses. 

e Elimination of rat burrows by blocking them with concrete, 

(ii) Trapping 

e It causes temporary reduction in the number of commensal rodents. 

e Various types of traps are cage traps, sherman traps, trigger or snap trap, break back traps. 

¢ Traps are usually baited with indigenous foods of the locality. 

e — Itis recommended that the number of traps laid should be at least 5% of human population. 

(iii) Rodenticides 
¢ They are of two main types - single dose (acute) and multiple dose (cumulative). 

© The former are lethal to the rat after a single feeding, while the latter require repeated feeding over a 

period of 3 more days. 
e Thecommonly used single dose (acute) poisons are Barium carbonate and zinc phosphide recommended 

for large scale use against rats. 

e The multiple dose (cumulative) poisons are warfarin, diphacinone, coumafury] and pindone. 
(iv) Fumigation 

e  Itis an effective method of destroying both rats and rat fleas. 
¢ The fumigants used are calcium cyanide, carbon disulfide, methyl bromide, sulphur dioxide etc 
e  Cyanogas (calcium cyanide) is prepared in powder form and is umped into rat 7 

foot pump called the "cyanogas pump". ena eas boar: 
(v) Chemosterilants 

e These are chemicals that can cause temporary or permanent sterility in either sex or both sexes.

Clinical Establishment | 

Clinical establishment means any hospital, maternity home, nursing home, dispensary, clinic, sanatorium 
or an institution by whatever name called that offers services, facilities requiring diagnosis, treatment or care for 
illness, injury, deformity, abnormality or pregnancy in any recognised system of medicine established

Women Empowerment 
“ee ee ore 

It is a process that leads women to realise their full potential, their rights to have access to opportunities, | 
resources and choices with the freedom of decision making both within and outside 

home. 

“National Policy for the Empowerment of Women (NPEW), was formulated in 2001, which laid down a 

comprehensive progressive policy for the advancement, development and empowerment of women with] 

appropriate policy prescriptions and strategies. 

Vision 
A society in which, women attain their full potential and are able to participate as equal partners in all 

spheres of life and influence the process of social change. 

Objective 

° Creating a conducive socio-cultural, economic and political environment to enable women enjoy their 

| fundamental rights and realize their full potential. 

. Elimination of all forms of violence against women through strengthening of policies. 

° Equal participation in the social, political and economic spheres including the institutions of governance and| 
decision making. 

. Improving and incentivizing access of women or girls to universal and quality education. 

. Developing a gender sensitive legal-judicial system. 

Scheme for empowerment of women 

e . .Beti Bachao Beti Padhao (BBBP) 

° Ujjawala Scheme 

° Pradhan Mantri Matru Vandana Yojana (PMMVY) 

. Swadhar Greh Scheme 

° Working Women Hostel Scheme. 

Gender Issue CM 10.9 

Despite a constitution that guarantees equal rights for men and women and decades of legislation, some 
deep-rooted gender discrimination in India takes a brutal toll on women’s lives. 

Problems faced by girl child include foeticide and infanticide ( before birth to 1 year of age) ,poor health 
attention (Nutrition Anemia, vitamin deficiency, Malnutrition etc),child marriage, low literacy level,Marital and 
Domestic violence etc 

National plan of action for children 2016 
| 

It is an initiative to further strengthen and activate the implementation and monitoring of national 1 

constituti 4 
onal and policy commitments and the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child. 

= 

Objectives of NPAC 2016 
A 

° Ensure equitable access to comprehensive and essential preventive, promotive, curative and rehabilitative ; 
health care of the highest standard, for all children before, during and after birth. i 

. Secure the right of every child to learning, knowledge, education, and development opportunity. tt 

° Create a caring, protective and safe environment for all children, to reduce their vulnerability in all situa- 
tions. a 

. Enable children to be actively involved in their own development and in all matters concerning and affe.c ! 
ing them.

important Public Health Events Of Last 5 Years 

Ayushman Bharat 

Ayushman Bharat comprises of two components, 

I. The first component pertains to creation of 1,50,000 Health and Wellness Centres (AB-HWCs) by 
upgrading the Sub Health Centres (SHCs) and rural and urban Primary Health Centres (PHCs), in 
both urban and rural areas, to bring health care closer to the community. 

I]. The second component is the Ayushman Bharat-Pradhan Mantri Jan ArogyaYojana (AB-PMJAY). 

Pradhan Mantri Surakshit Matritva Abhiyan (PMSMA) : Since inception, more than 3.02 crore antenatal 
check-ups have been conducted and 25.46 lakh high risk pregnancies have been identified under PMSMA 
across States/ UTs till 4" December 2021. 

LaQshya : It aims to improve the quality of care in Labour’ Room and Maternity Operation Theatres to 
ensure that pregnant women receive respectful and quality care during delivery and immediate postpartum 
MusQan — Quality improvement initiative of Child Health services : The initiative will be focusing on 
improving the quality parameters for ensuring safety and availability of infrastructure, equipment, supplies, 
skilled human resources,-clinical protocols, evidence based practices etc. at public health facilities, 



_— 

. Pradhan MantriSwasthya Suraksha Yojana (PMSSY) : The Pradhan MantriSwasthya Suraksha Yojana 
(PMSSY) envisages creation of tertiary healthcare capacity in medical education, research and clinica] Care, 
in the underserved areas of the country. 

° Reproductive, Maternal, Newborn, Child, Adolescent Health Plus Nutrition (RMNCAH+N) The 

RMNCAH#N strategy is based on provision of delivering comprehensive healthcare through six key areas. 
Reproductive, Maternal, Neonatal, Child, Adolescent Health along with Nutrition. This strategy is guided by 

fundamental principles such as universal care, ensuring of entitlement or essential services and maintaining 
accountability. 

EVENTS IMPORTANT TO HEALTH OF COMMUNITY CM 20.3} 

World Tuberculosis Day: 24" March is World Tuberculosis Day. 

Each year we commemorate World TB Day to raise public awareness about the devastating health, social 
and economic consequences of tuberculosis (TB) and to step up efforts to end the global TB epidemic. bs 

World Health Day: 7" April is World Health Day. 
7 

It is celebrated annually and every year draws attention to a specific health topic of concern to people ¢ 
, “1 

over the world. 

Malaria Day: 25" April is World Malaria Day. | 

“World Malaria Day” is an occasion to highlight the need for continued investment and sustained politi all 
commitment for malaria prevention and control. iz 

ey 
a 

Suicide prevention day a 

The theme for "World Suicide Prevention Day" or the years 2024-26 is "Changing the Narrative on Suicide’} 
with an associated call to action: "Start the Conversation". . 

The 10" September of every year aims to focus attention on the issue, reduces stigma and raises aware ess|

HEALTH HAZARDS OF HEALTH CARE WASTE 

(i) Hazards from infections waste and sharps 

e Pathogens in infectious waste may enter the human body through a puncture, abrasion or cut in the 
skin, through mucous membranes by inhalation or ingestion 

Example : HIV, Hepatitis B & C virus. 

(ii) | Hazards from chemicals and pharmaceutical waste 

e¢ Many of the chemical and pharmaceuticals used in health care establishments are toxic genotoxic 
corrosive, flammable, reactive, explosive or shock sensitive. 

e Disinfectants are particularly important members of this group.

TREATMENT & DISPOSAL TECHNOLOGIES FOR HEALTH CARE WASTE : 
(i) Incineration : It is a "High temperature dry oxidation" process, it leads to significant reduction in waste| 7 

volume & weight (upto 70-80%) ee 

Characteristics of wastes suitable for incineration : 

Low heating volume, combustion matter > 60%, moisture content < 30%, etc. 

e Types of Incinerators 

(a) Double chamber pyrolytic wi 

(b) Single chamber pyrolytic | 

(c) Rotary kilns I 

e | Temperature in an incinerator > 1200°C | | = 

Primary chamber 800° + 50°C 

Secondary chamber 1050° + 50°C | 

e Waste types not to be incinerated 

Pressurized gas containers 

Reactive chemical waste 

Halogenated plastics 

Silver / Radiographic / Photographic wastes/Sealed ampoules/ Waste with high mercury. : 4 
(ii) Chemical Disinfection 

° Chemicals are added to waste to kill or inactivate the 

(iii) | Wet and Dry Thermal Treatment 

e Wet thermal treatment : It is based on exposure of shredded 
pressure steam, and is similar to the autoclave Sterilization 



(iv) Microwave Irradiation 

Most microorganisms are destroyed by the action of microwave of a frequency of about 2450 MHZ and 
a wave length of 12.24 nm. 

e The water contained within the waste is rapidly heated by the microwaves and the infectious 
components are destroyed by heat conduction. 

(v) land Disposal 
e There are two types of disposal land - open dumps and sanitary landfills; 

Health care waste should not be deposited on or around open dumps. 
Sanitary landfills are designed to have atleast four advantages over open dumps: 
(i) Geological isolation of waste from environment 
(ii) Appropriate engineering preparation before the site is ready to accept waste 
(ili) Staff present on site to control operations 
(iv) Organized deposits and daily coverage of waste. 

(vi) Inertization 

Mixing biomedical waste with cement and other substances before disposal, So as to minimize risk of 

toxic substances contained in waste to contaminate ground / surface water. 
A typical composition of mixture is : 
> 65% pharmaceutical waste 
> 15% lime 

> 15% cement 

> 5% water

CHAPTER 

14 | Disaster Management 

DISASTER cM 13.1 
Definition 

According to Disaster Management Act 2005 of India "Disaster means a catastrophe, mishap, calamity = 
grave occurrence in any area, arising from natural or man made causes or accidents or negligence which 

result in substantial loss of life or humans suffering or damage to, and destruction of property, or damage 
to, or degradation of, environment, and is of such a nature or magnitude as to be beyond the coping capacity 
of the community of the affected area". 

Feature of Disaster 

Most commonly reported disease in post disaster phase is gastroenteritis. 

Most practical and effective strategy of disease prevention and control in post disaster phase is "supplying 

safe drinking water and proper disposal of excreta". 

Foremost step for disease prevention and control in post disaster phase is chlorination of all water bodies. 

Other common deficiencies include scurvy (Vitamin C), anaemia (iron) and pellagra (Vitamin B, - niacin). 

A common micronutrient deficiency in disasters is vitamin A deficiency. 

Types of Disasters 

According to Speed of Onset 

Disaster 

' 
Slow onset disaster Rapid onset disaster 

le Adisaster that unfolds with development process q A disaster that is triggered by an 

le Hazard can be left as an ongoing stress for many instantaneous shock 
days, months or even years ° Example : Earthquakes, cyclones, floods, 

Example : Drought, pest infections volcanic eruptions. 

Natural Hazards : Disaster information management system of Deslnventar classifies disaster arising from 
natural hazards into five major groups: 

{i) Geophysical 

(a) Earthquakes / Mass movement of earth material 

(b) Volcano 

(c) Tsunami 

(ii) Hydrological 

(a) Flood - flash flood 

(b) Landslides - Avalanche, debriflow, mud flow, rockfall 

(c) Wave action- Wind generated surface waves of water over oceans, rivers or lakes. 



(iii) Meteorological 

(a) Cyclone, storm surge, tornado, convective storm, High 
wind. 

(b) Cold wave, derecho 

(c) Extreme temperature, fog, freeze, frost. 

(d) Lighting, heavy rains 

(e) Sand storm, dust-storm 

(f) Snow, ice, winter storm, blizzard 

{iv) Climatological 

(a) Drought i 
(b) Extreme hot / cold conditions 

(c) Forest wildfire 

(d) Glacial lake outburst 

(v) Biological 

(a) Epidemics : Viral, bacterial, parasitic, fungal or prion infections. 

(b) Insect infestations 

Human Induced Disasters:  A serious disruption triggered by a human induced hazard causing human, 
material, economic, or environmental losses, which exceed the ability of those affected to cope. 

(i) Accidental : Transportation accident, collapse of building, Drowning, explosions, fire etc. 

(ii) Civil Disturbances : Riots and demonstrations 

(iii) Warfare: C onventional warfare (Bombardment, blockage or siege); Non-conventional warfare (Nuclear, 
Biological and chemical warfare) 

(iv) Refugees : Forced movements of large no. of people across frontiers. 

(v) Examples : Bhopal Gas disaster (1984) 

Uphaar cinema fire in Delhi (1997) 

Mumbai terror attack (2008) 

DISASTER MANAGEMENT 

. There are 3 fundamental aspects of disaster management 

(i) Disaster response 

(ii) Disaster preparedness; and 
A. 

(iii) Disaster mitigation 
. ~ _, Ma“na gement 

(i) Disaster Impact and Response 

e The management of mass casualties can be further divided into 

search and rescue, first aid, triage and stabilization of victim
s, 

Hospital treatment and redistribution of patients to other 

hospitals if necessary. ; 
Figure 

e _ Search, Rescue and First Aid 

+ Most immediate help comes from the uninjured survivors. 

+ The need for search, rescue
 and first aidi s so great that

 or 

| meet only a small fracti
on of the demand. 

Cr ————— 



Field Care 

Most injured persons converge spontaneously to health facilities 

Bed availability and surgical services should be maximized 
Provisions should be made for food & shelter 
Inquiry centre should be established to respond inquiries from patients relatives & friends. 

Priority should be given to victim's identification and adequate mortuary space should be provided. 
Triage 

Ri aeree ‘ 
+ It consists of rapidly classifying the injured on the basis of the severity of their injuries and th 

likelihood of their survival with prompt medical intervention. 

+ The principle of "first come, first treated" is not followed in mass emergencies. 

+ High priority is granted to victims whose immediate or long term p
rognosis can be dramatically 

affected by simple intensive care. 

+ Moribund patients who require a great deal of attention, with questionable benefits have the 

lowest priority. 

+ Four colour code system of triage : 

» Red - High priority treatment or transfer 

® Yellow - Medium priority 

>» Green - Ambulatory patients 

» Black - Dead or moribund patients. 

+ Triage should be carried out at the site of disaster, in order to determine transportation priority 
and admission to the hospitals. 

+ Local health workers should be taught the principles of triage as part of disaster training 

+ Persons with minor or moderate injuries should be treated at their own homes and seriously 
injured should be transported to hospitals with specialized treatment facilities. 

Tagging 

+ All patients should be identified with tags stating their name, age, place of origin, triage category, 
diagnosis and initial treatment. 

Identification of Dead 

+ Care of the dead include : 

> Removal of the dead from the disaster scene 

» Shifting to the mortuary 

>» Identification 

> Reception of bereaved relatives 

+ If human bodies contaminate streams, wells, or other water sources as in floods etc they can 
transmit gastroenteritis or food poisoning to survivors. 

Relief Phase 

+ This phase begins when assistance from outside starts to reach the disaster area. 
+ The type and quantity of humanitarian relief supplies are usually determined by two main factors. 

(i) The type of disaster, since distinct events have different effect on the population. 
(ii) The type and quantity of supplies available locally. 

+ a He es 



+ Theraree  four principal components in managing humanitarian supplies: 

(i) | Acquisition of supplies 

(ii) Transportation 

(iii) Storage 

(iv) Distribution 

Epidemiologic surveillance and disease control 

+ Disaster can increase the transmission of communicable diseases through following mechanisms: 

(i) Overcrowding and poor sanitation in temporary resettlement 

(ii) Population displacement may lead to introduction of communicable diseases to which either 
the migrant or indigenous population are susceptible. 

(iii) Ecological changes may favour breeding of vectors and increase the vector population density 

(iv) Disruption of routine control programme as funds and personnel are usually diverted to relief 
work. 

Vaccination 

+ The WHO does not recommend typhoid and cholera vaccine in routine use in endemic areas 

+ Supplying safe drinking water and proper disposal of excreta continue to be the most practical and 

effective strategy. 

+ Mass vaccination against tetanus is usually unnecessary; Best protection is maintenance of a high level 

of immunity in the general population by routine vaccination before disaster. 

+ Natural disasters may affect the maintenance of on going eradication programmes against polio and 

measles; disruption of these programmes should be monitored closely. 

Nutrition 

The immediate steps for ensuring that the food relief programme will be effective include: 

(i) Assessing the food supplies after the disaster 

(ii) Gauging the nutritional needs of the affected population 

(iii) Calculating the daily food rations and need for large population groups. 

(iv) Monitoring the nutritional status of the affected population. 

Rehabilitation 

+ The final phase in a disaster should lead to restoration of the pre disaster conditions. 

+ Water supply : A survey of all public water supplies should be made; this include distribution system 

and water sources. 

> The first priority of ensuring water quality in emergency situation is chlorina
tion; it is the best way 

of disinfecting water. 

> The existing and new water sources require the following protec
tion measures. 

(i) Restrict access to people and animal 

(ii) Ensure adequate excreta disposal at a safe dis
tance 

(iii) Prohibit bathing, washing and animal husbandary, upstre
am of intake points in rivers and streams.

Of special concern are dengue fever and malaria, leptospirosis and rat bite fiver, typhus and plague. 
(ii) | Disaster Mitigation in Health Sector 

+ Measures designed to either prevent hazards from causing emergency or to lessen the effect of 
emergency. 

+ These measures include flood mitigation work, appropriate land use planning, impr oved building 
codes, and reduction or protection of vulnerable population & structures. 

+ In most cases mitigation measures aim to reduce the vulnerability of the system. 

+ Mitigation complements the disaster preparedness and disaster response activities. 

(iii) | Disaster Preparedness 

+ Emergency preparedness is "a programme of long term development activities whose goals are to 

strengthen the overall capacity and capability of a country to manage efficiently all types of emergency. 

It should bring about an orderly transition from relief through recovery and back to sustained 

development". 

+ The objectives of disaster preparedness is to ensure that appropriate systems, procedures and resources 

are in place to provide prompt effective assistance to disaster victims. 

+ Disaster preparedness is an ongoing multisectoral activity. 

+ Tasks carried out by a variety of sectors of disaster preparedness system are : 

> Risk evaluation of area from past experience 

> Adopt standards and regulations. 

> Organize communication, information, warning system 

> Develop public education programmes. 

> Coordination with meteorology, health system, media 

> Training, simulation exercises 

> Ensure coordination and response mechanism.

|MAN MADE DISASTERS CM 13.31 

There are many disasters which have large elements of human causation either accidental or intended. 

° These can also be divided into there categories: 

(a) Sudden disasters such as Bhopal Gas tragedy in India; About 2 million people were exposed to gas 

leaving about 3,000 dead. 

(b) Insidious disaster, such as insidious chemical exposure and insidious radiation exposure, as in nuclear| 

weapons production factories. 

(c) Wars and civil conflicts; Ex : attack on twin buildings of world trade centre in New York in which about 
6000 people lost their lives. 

° Since world war II, there have been about 127 wars and 21.8 million war-related deaths involving more than 
90% of civilians. 

* __ The public health response to man made disaster is the primary prevention i.e., prevention of occurrence of 
the disaster. ) 
People around the world have turned towards efforts to stop the arms race and prevent nuclear war.

of natural disaster.
