. | 

National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA) is the lead agency responsible for preparation of disaster| 

management plans and the execution of functions at National level. 

Disaster management Act 2005 provides for setting of NDMA at national level and SDMA at state level, 

: Emergency medical relief using coordinates activities related to mental health under ministry of health ang 

family welfare. 

' A part of International decade for natural disaster reduction, every year second Wednesday of October 

designated as world disaster reduction day. 

. Indian meteorological department plays key role in forwarning the disaster. 

Functions of NDMA 

i. Lay down policies on disaster management. 

2. Approves National plan. 

3; Lay down guidelines to be followed by State authorities in drawing up State Plan. 

4. Lays down guidelines to be followed by the different Ministries or departments of Government of India for 
the purpose of integrating measures for prevention of disaster. 

Provision of funds for the purpose of mitigation.

ERGONOMICS 
d 'nomos', meaning The term "Ergonomics" is derived from the greek word 'ergon, meaning work an 

principles or laws. 

It simply means "Fitting the job to the worker" 

The object of ergonomics is "To achieve the best mutual adjustment of man and his
 work, for the improvement 

of human efficiency and well-being". 

The application of ergonomics has made a significant contribution to redu
cing industrial accidents and to 

the overall health and efficiency of the workers".

Silicosis is the major cause of permanent disability and mortality. 

It is caused by inhalation of dust containing free silica or silicon dioxide 
(SiO,). 

. Silicosis is caused by exposure to crystalline silica, which originates from 
drilling, Crushing, Braking, Grinding, Hammering, and sand blasting of 
earth's crust. 

: The incidence of silicosis depends upon the chemical composition of the 
dust, size of the particles, duration of exposure and individual susceptibility. 

° Incubation period may vary from a few months up to 6 years of exposure. 

= Silicosis was made a notifiable disease under the Factories Act 1948, and the mines Act 1952. | 

° Clinical Features 

Q = Onset of disease is insidious. 

Q Early manifestations are irritant cough, dyspnea on exertion and pain in the chest. 

Q Silicosis is characterized by a dense "Nodular" fibrosis, the nodules ranging from 3 to 4 mm in diameter. 

Q Chest x-ray shows "snow-storm" appearance in the lung field. 

Q Silicosis is progressive 

Q ___ Silicotics are prone to pulmonary tuberculosis, a condition called "Silico-tuberculosis". 

° Silicosis can be controlled by 

(a) Rigorous dust control measures. 

(b) Regular physical examination of workers. 

e Fibrotics changes that have already take place cannot be reversed. 

Anthracosis 

€ Anthracosis is due to inhalation of coal dust. 

& There are two general phases in coal miners pneumoconiosis 

(i) Simple pneumoconiosis (require about 12 years of work exposure for its development). 

(ii) Progressive massive fibrosis: It causes severe respiratory disability and frequently results in premature 

death. 

€ The risk of death among coal miners has been nearly twice that of the general population. 

Byssinosis 

€ Byssinosis is due to inhalation of cotton fibre dust over long period of time.. 

e It is also known as Monday fever, cotton worker's lung, brown lung disease etc. 

« Symptoms are chronic cough, progressive dyspnoea, Chronic bronchitis and emphysema. 

Bagassosis 
e It is a form of hyper sensitivity pneumonitis, which is caused due to inhalation of sugarcane fibre vaste 

(Called bagasse); it is a form of extrinsic allergic alveolitis.

Asbestos is used in the manufacture of asbestos cement, fire proof textiles, roof tiling, brake lining etc. 

Asbestos fibres are usually from 20 to 500 pt in length and 0.5 to 50 1 in diameter. 

° Asbestos enters the body by inhalation. 

The dust deposited in the lungs causes pulmonary fibrosis, carcinoma of bronchus, mesothelioma of ple

6 

or peritoneum and cancer of gastrointestinal tract. 

° Most dangerous type of asbestos is amphibole. 

° The disease does not usually appear until after 5 to 10 years of exposure.

~ Persistent cough. 

~~ Sputum shows "Asbestos bodies". 

ed Chest x-ray shows ground glass appearance in the lower 2/3rd of the lung field. 

° Preventive Measures: 

(i) Use of safer types of asbestos. 

(ii) Substitution of other insulant (glass fibre, plastic foam). 

(iii) Rigorous dust control. 

(iv) Periodic examination of workers. 

(v) Continuing research.

All lead compounds are toxic - lead arsenate, lead oxide and lead carbonate are most dangerous, lead ; 

sulphide is least toxic. , 

The body store of lead in the average adult population is about 150-400 mg and blood levels average about 

25 ug / 100 ml. 

® An increase to 70 yg / 100 ml blood is generally associated with clinical symptoms. 

Lead is used widely because of its properties. 

(i) Low boiling point. 

(ii) Anticorrosive. 

(ili) Easily oxidised. 

(iv) Mixes with other metals easily to form alloys. 

Modes of absorption 

(i) Inhalation (Most common mode) 

(ii). Ingestion (Less common) 

(iii) Skin (Absorption through skin occurs only in respect of the organic compounds of lead). 

Clinical features 

(i) Facial pallor: Earliest and most consistent sign. 

(ii) Anaemia: Microcytic hypochromic 

(iii) Punctate basophilia or basophilic stippling of RBC. 

(iv) Burtonian line: blue line on gums. 

(v) Lead colic: Constipation. 

(vi) Lead palsy: Wrist drop or foot drop. 

(vii) Lead encephalopathy.

stippling of red cells) ¢ Lead in urine > 0.8 mg/L 

| 
e A useful screening test is coproporphyrin in urine (CPU) 

. Preventive measures: 

(i) Substitution of lead by less toxic material. 

(ii) Local exhaust ventilation, personal protection. 

(iii) Working atmosphere (Lead concentration is kept below 2.0 mg per 10 cu.metres) _

Nearly 75% occupational cancers are skin cancers. 

Skin Cancer: An occupational hazard among gas workers, oil refiners, dye stuff makers, road makers, fuel 
oils, etc. 

0 Predominantly squamous cell carcinoma. 

Lung Cancer: Arsenic, beryllium and isopropyl oil are suspected carcinogens. 

0 More than nine-tenths of ]ung cancer are attributed to tobacco smoking, air pollution and occupation 

exposure. 

Cancer Bladder : It is caused by carcinogens like beta napthylamines, benzidine, para amino diphenyl, 

auramine and magenta. 

Leukaemia: Benzol is a dangerous chemical and is used as a solvent in many industries. 

Characteristics of Occupational Cancers 

(i) They appear after prolonged exposure. 

(ii) Disease may develop even after the cessation of exposure. 

(iii) Incidence is earlier than that for cancer in general. 

(iv) Localization of cancer is constant in any one occupation. 

(v) The period between exposure and development of disease may be as long as 10 to 2
5 years. 

Control Measures 

(i) Elimination or control of industrial carcinogens. 

(ii) Medical examination 

(iii) Inspection of factories. 

(iv) Notification. 

(v) Licensing of establishment. 

(vi) Personal hygiene measures. 

| (vii) Health education.

Prevention of Occupational Dermatitis

Radiation Hazards 

e A number of industries use radium and other radio active substances. 
e Exposure to radium occur in mining of radio active ores; 
e Exposure to ultra violet rays occur in arc and other electric welding processes. 
« Exposure to infrared rays occur in welding, glass blowing, foundry work and other processes.

Preventive Measures 

Avoiding inhalation, swallowing or direct contact with the skin. 
Shielding should be used in case of x-rays. 
Monitoring of employee at intervals. 
Protective clothing and adequate ventilation. 
Periodic examination of workers. 

Pregnant women should not be allowed to work in places where there is continuous exposure. _

‘. 

(i) Zoonotic diseases: Brucellosis, Anthrax, Leptospirosis, Tetanus, Tuberculosis, Q fever etc. 

(ii) Accidents: Due to increased use of agricultural machinery; insect bite and snake bite. 

(iii) Toxic hazards: Fertilizers, insecticides or pesticides. 

(iv) Physical hazards: Heat (Head stroke, heat exhaustion),
 weather (violent rain, drought, flash floods, 

excessive noise and vibration etc. 

(v) Respiratory Disease: Byssinosis, Bagassosis, farmer's
 lung and occupational lung disease. 

Sickness Absenteeism 

Sickness absenteeism is a useful index in industry to asse
ss the state of health of workers, and their physical, 

mental and social well being. 

Sickness absenteeism is an important health problem in industry. 

Rate of absenteeism reported in India: 8-10 days per worker per year. 

Causes 

(i) Economic causes: Misuse of sick leaves; 

(ii) Social causes: Wedding, festivals, repair and
 maintenance of ancestral house, visiting vil

lages during 

sowing and harvest season. 

(iii) Medical cause: Example: Occupational accidents
; respiratory and alimentary illnesses. 

(iv) Non-occupational causes: Example: Nutritional
 disorders, alcoholism, drug addiction etc. 

Methods of Reducing Sickness Absen
teeism 

(i) Good factory management and p
ractices. 

(ii) Adequate pre-placement examin
ation. 

(iii) Good human relations 

(iv) Application of ergonomics

PREVENTION OF OCCUPATIONAL DISEASES CM 11.3 

Measures for the prevention of occupational diseases are, 

(i) Medical measures 

(ii) Engineering measures. 

(iii) Legislative. 

Medical Measures 

(i) Pre-placement Examination 

The purpose of pre-placement examination is to place the right man in the right job, so that the worker 
can perform his duties efficiently without detriment to his health. 

Pre-placement examination is done at the time of employment and include worker's medical, family, 
occupational and social history; physical examination, radiological examination, and special tests for 
endemic diseases. 

(ii) Periodic Examination 

Many diseases of occupational origin require months or even years for their development. 
Examinations held at appropriate intervals to test worker's physical and mental efficiency and to detect 
any departure from health at the earliest. 

¢ Major objective is early diagnosis and prompt treatment. 

© Frequency of periodic examination 

¢ Daily - For dichromate exposure 

¢ Monthly - For lead, dye stuff exposure 

¢ Every 2 months - Radiation industry 

* Annual - Most occupational exposures. 
(iii) Medical and Health Care Services 

Medical care of occupational diseases is a basic function of an occupational health service. 
¢ In India, the ESI scheme provide medical care not only for the workers but also to his family. 

(iv) Notification 

xy is to initiate measures for prevention and protection. i | 
and to investigate the working conditions and other circumstance 
have caused occupational diseases, =: . Se 

fl . Pil 
a” he tia 



[(v) Supervision of Working Environment Ria eRe ae ae ; 
°-o» The physician should pay frequent visits to factory in order to acquaint himself with the various , 

of the working environment such as temperature, lighting, ventilation, humidity, noise, cubic space, air 
pollution etc which have an important effect on the health and welfare of the workers. 

(vi) Maintenance and Analysis of Records 

* — Proper records, are essential for the planning, development and efficient operation of an occupational
 

health service. ; 

(vii) Health Education and Counselling 

¢ The purpose of health education is to assit the worker in his process of adjustment to the working, 

home and community environment. 

* all the risks involved in industry in which he is employed and the measures to be taken for personal 

protection should be explained to him. 

Engineering Measures 

(i) Design of building 

° The type of floor, walls height, ceiling, roof, doors and windows, cubic spac
es are all matters which 

should receive attention in the original plan of the building. 

(ii) Good house keeping 

¢ —_ Itinclude general cleanliness, ventilation, lighting, washing, food arrangement a
nd general maintenance. 

¢ Good house keeping is a fundamental requirement for the control or el
imination of occupational 

hazards. 

(iii) General ventilation 

¢ Good general ventilation decreases the air borne hazards to the workers, especial
ly hazards from dust 

and gases. 

© Indian factories act has prescribed a minimum of 500 cu.ft of air
 space for each worker. 

(iv) Mechanization 

The plant should be mechanized to the fullest possible ext
ent to reduce the hazard of contact with 

harmful substances. 

© Dermatitis can be prevented if hand mixing is replaced by mechan
ical devices. 

(v) Substitution 

¢ It means replacement of a harmful material by a harmless one, or one of lesser toxicity.
 

Example: Zinc or iron paints can be used in place of harmful lead pain
ts. 

(vi) Dust 

© Dust can be controlled at the point of origin by water sprays 

Example: Wet drilling of rock. 

(vii) Enclosure 

* Enclosing the harmful materials and processes will prevent the escape of dust and fumes into the 
factory atmosphere. 

Example: Grinding machinery 

lsolation 

4 ' * To isolate the offensive process in a separate building so that workers are not directly connected with| 
the operation and are saved from exposure.

THE EMPLOYEES STATE INSURANCE ACT, 1948 
CM 11.2 

| ae ESI act is an important measure of social security and health
 insurance in India. 

(i) Scope 

| © The act covers all the factories in India excluding mines, 

defence, railways. 

¢ Coverage : Non seasonal factories employing 10 or more 

persons. 

¢ Shops, hotels restaurants, cinemas, mewspaper 

establishment etc employing 20 or more people. 
Employees’ State Insurance 

* Private medical and educational institutions employing 

20 or more persons in certain states/UT's. 
Scheme (ESIC) 

* It covers all employees getting income upto rupees 21,000/ month. Figure 

* — It covers all states except Manipur, Sikkim, Arunachal Pradesh, Mizoram etc. 

fii) Administration 

* The union minster of labour is the chairman of ESI corporation. 

(iii) Finance 

* Employer contribute 3.25% of total wage bill 

* Employee contribute 0.75% of wages (those earning < Rs. 100/- per day exempted) 

% _ * — State and central government share medical expenditure in ratio of 1 : 7.

+ Legislation may be directed at controlling the manufacture, distribution prescription, pric

+ The anti smoking measures suggested are : 
(a) Prohibition of sale of tobacco products to minors 

(b) Restriction on the sale of cigarettes from automatic vending machines. 

(c) Prohibition of smoking in schools and in other places by young people. 

(d) Prohibition of smoking in public 

(e) Prohibition of cigarette advertisement

le Educational Approach 

+ Common approaches have included educational programmes for school children and public

e Community Approach 

+ There should be a strong emphasis on action at the community level to prevent drug ab
use.

s 

every day lives and actions, and contributes to emphasis on strengthening primary

Treatment 

° Treatment cannot take place unless the individual attends for treatment. 

° Unformulated drug takers, as a rule have little or no motivation to undergo treatment. 

e First step in management of drug addition is medical care, which include: 

(i) Identification of drug addicts and their motivation for detoxication. 

(ii) Detoxication (require hospitalization) 

(iii) Post detoxication counselling and follow up (based on clinic and home visits) 

(iv) Rehabilitation 

Rehabilitation 
* The rehabilitation of a formed drug user, is in most cases a long and difficult process. 

¥ Relapses are very frequent

Sleep or Appetite Changes : Dramatic sleep and apetite changes or decline in personal care. 

Mood Changes : Rapid or dramatic shifts in emotions or depressed feelings, greater irritability. 

Withdrawal : Recent social withdrawal and loss of interest in activities previously enjoyed. 

Drop in Functioning : An unusual drop in functioning, at school, work or social activities, such as sports, 

failing in school or difficulty performing familiar tasks. 

Problems Thinking : Problems with concentration, memory or logical thought and speech are hard to explain. 

Increased Sensitivity: Heightened sensitivity to sights, sounds, smells or touch, avoidance of over stimulating 

situations. 

Apathy : Loss of initiative or desire to participate in any activity. 

Feeling Disconnected: A vague feeling of being disconnected from oneself or one's surroundings, a sense of 

unreality. 

illogical Thinking : Unusual or exaggerated beliefs about personal powers to understand meanings or 

influence events, illogical or magical thinking typical of childhood in an adult. 

Nervousness : Fear or suspiciousness of others or a strong nervous feeling. 

Unusual Behaviour : Odd, uncharacteristic, peculiar behaviour. 

Changes in School or Work : Increased absenteeism, worsening performance, difficulties in relationships with 

peers and co-workers. 

If a person is experiencing several at one time and symptoms are causing serious problem in ability to study, 

work or relate to others. 

People with suicidal thoughts or intent or thoughts of harming others, need immediate attention.

(iil) Sample Registration System (SRS) 
It was initiated in the mid 1960's to provide reliable estimates of birth and death rates at the national & 
state levels. 

SRS is a dual record system consisting of continuous enumeration of births and deaths by an enumerator 
and an independent survey every 6 months by an investigator - Supervisor. 

It is a major source of health information 

SRS now covers the entire country. 

The main objective of SRS is to provide reliable estimate of birth rate, death rate, infant mortality rate, 
under five and adult mortality etc. 

(iv) Notification of Diseases 

The primary purpose of notification is to effect prevention and / or control of the disease. 

Diseases notifiable to WHO under International Health Regulation (IHR) are: Cholera, plague, and 

yellow fever. 

loose borne typhus, relapsing fever, polio, influenza, malaria, rabies and salmonellosis are subject to 
international surveillance. 

The concept of notification has been extended to many non communicable diseases and conditions 
notably cancer, congenital malformations, mental illness, stroke and handicapped persons. 

Limitations of notification of diseases 

+ Itcovers only a small part of the total sickness in the community 

+ The system suffers from a good deal of under reporting 

+ Many cases especially a typical and subclinical cases escape notification due to non-recognition. 

(v) Hospital Records 

Hospital data constitute a basic and primary source of information about diseases prevalent in the 
community 

Hospital] data provide information on the following aspects 

Geographic sources of patient 

Age and sex distribution of disease 

Distribution of diagnosis 

Association between different diseases 

Period between disease and hospital admission 

Cost of hospital care 

Duration of hospital stay 

Drawbacks of hospital data are 

+ They constitute only the 'tip of the iceberg' (mild cases may not attend hospital). 

+ Admission policy may vary from hospital to hospital. 

+ Population served by a hospital cannot be defined

(ix) Population Survey 

e The term “health survey" is used for survey relating to any aspect of
 health - morbidity, mo a ity, 

nutritional status etc. 

° | When the main variable to be studied is diseases suffered by the people the survey is referred toa 

"morbidity survey". 

e Types of survey covered under health surveys are 

+ Survey for evaluating the health status of a population. 

+ Survey for investigation of factors affecting health and disease 

+ Survey relating to administration of health services. 

e Survey methods: Health survey can be broadly classified into 4 types 
+ Health interview survey (face to face) 

+ Health examination survey 

+ Health records survey 

+ Mailed questionnaire survey. 

e The survey is carried out by teams consisting of doctors, technicians and interviewers.

Map Diagram/Spot Map 

It is prepared to show geographical distribution of frequencies of characteristics (eg. Diseases) 

Each spot (dot) marks one frequency. 

Mainly shows local variations in a disease. 

Scatter/Dot Diagram 

It is also known as "correlation diagram’. 

It is used to depict "correlation (relationship) between 2 quantitative variables". 

If the dots cluster round a straight line, it shows evidence of a relationship of a linear nature. 
If there is no such cluster, it is probable that there is no relationship between the variables.

The word “average" implies a median and mode. 

‘ \ e mean, 

There are several kinds of averages, of which the commonly

Mean 

“5 It is the sum of all observations divided 
by the number of observations 

+ Mean is denoted by sign x. 

+ The operation of adding together is called 
summation and is denoted by sign 2 

or 5. 

4 
x= ux ;n = no of observations. 

’ 
+ Advantage: It is easy to calculate and understand. 

+ Disadvantage: sometimes it may be unduly influenced by abnormal values in 
the distribution like ave ras 

number of children born to a woman in certain place was found to be 4.76 which never occur in reality. 

; 
Median 

+ It is that value which divide the complete data set into two equal parts. 
a 

+ It is the middle most value of the data when arranged in ascending or descending order. 

+ When total observations are an odd number, there is single middle value. 

+ When total observations are an even number, there are two middle values; then median is calcula ed 
i? i 

° *. . 

taking mean of these two middle observations. " 

+ Advantages of median are as follows: 

e Median is not affect by extreme high and low values. 7 gi 

e Median is often used for non-normal distributions wherein, it helps to convey the middle most ‘a 

Mode Re 

+ The most commonly occurring value or the most often repeated value is mode. : "i 
+ Advantages of mode are that it is easy to understand, and is not affected by the extreme items. 
+ Disadvantages are that the exact location is often uncertain and is often not clearly defined

Standard Normal Curve 

+ The standard normal curve is a smooth, bell shaped, perfectly symmetrical curve, based on an infinitely 

large number of observation. 

+ The total area of curve is 1; its mean is zero; and its standard deviation is 1. 

+ The mean, median and mode all coincide 

° The distance of a value (x) from the mean (x) of the curve in units of standard deviation is called "relative 
deviate or standard normal variate", and is usually denoted by 'Z'.

Selection of a subset of individuals from within a statistical population to estimate characteristics of the

imple Random Sampling 

Every unit of population has equal and known chance of being selected 

It is also known as "unrestricted random sampling". 

aie Applicable for small, homogenous and readily available population. 

2 e Methods of simple random sampling: 

(i) Lottery method 

(ii) Random number of tables 

(iii) Computer softwares. 

je Used in clinical trials. 

__ |Systematic Random Sampling 

| 3 je This is done by picking every 5" or 10" unit at regular intervals 

i | Sampling interval (k) = total number of units in population / total number of units in sample 

/e Applicable for large, non homogenous population where complete list of individuals is available. 

_ |Stratified Random Sampling 

« Non homogenous population is converted to homogenous groups / classes (strata); sample is drawn from 

each strata at random, in proportion to its size. 

« Applicable for large non-homogenous population. 

€ This method is particularly useful where one is interested in analysing the data by a certain characteristic of 

the population, viz Hindus, Christians, Muslims, age-groups etc as we know these groups are not equally 

distributed in the population. 

Multistage Random Sampling 

e It is done in successive stages: each successive sampling unit is nested in the previous sampling unit. 

je This method is useful in situations where a large population is to be covered. 

the Advantage: introduces flexibility in sampling 

° For example, in large country surveys, states are chosen, then districts, then villages, then every 10" persons 

in village as final sampling unit. 

{Cluster Random Sampling 

1b Applicable when units of population are natural groups or clusters. 

1° Use: Evaluation of immunization coverage 

4 Clusters are heterogenous within themselves but homogenous with respect to each other 

\° Accuracy : Low error rate of only 5%. 

{® __ Limitations:  Clusters cannot be compared with each other. 

a Convenience Sampling 

Patients are selected, in part or in whole, at the convenience of the researcher; 

For example, standing at a shopping mall and selecting shoppers as they walk by to fill out a survey

CHI-SQUARE TEST 
. It is a non-parametric test of significance. £5 

e It is used to test significance of association between 2 or more qualitative e. sz 

° __ Before applying the chi-square test, the data needs to meet a few requ
irements and assumptions as foll Ws; 

(i) Both the variables between which 
association is to be tested should be

 categorical. 

(ii) At least 80% of expected cell values should be more than 5. 

(iii) None of the expected cell value should be less than 1. 

Applications of chi square test; 

(i) Test of proportions 

(ii) Test of association 

(iii) Test of goodness of fit.

Sampling error 

If we take repeated samples from the same population or universe, the results obtained from one sample will 

differ to some extent from the results of another sample; this type of variation from one sample to another is 

called sampling error. 

The factors that influence the sampling error are 

(i) Size of the sample 

(ii) The natural variability of the individual reading 

As the size of the sample increases, sampling error will decrease. 

Power of a Test 

It is the probability of rejecting a null hypothesis when a predetermined clinically significant difference is 

indeed present. 

Confidence Interval 

It is the interval within which a parameter value is expected to lie with certain confidence level, as could be 
revealed by repeated sample. 

Confidence Level 

It is the level of hope or expectation fixed at a sufficiently high level while dealing with samples, to ensure 
high reliability.

COMMUNICATION PROCESS 

Communication can be regarded as a two way process of exchanging or shaping ideas, feelings and 

information. 

Components of Communication Process 

(i Sender (Source) 

i Receiver (Audience) 

ii) Message (Content) 

(iv) Channels (Medium) 

(v) |Feedback (Effect) 

Sender 

The sender (communicator) is the originator of the message; to be an effective communicator, he must know; 

His objectives, clearly defined 

Interests and needs of his audience 

His message 

Channel of communication 

His professional abilities & limitations 

Receiver 

All communications must have an audience, this may be a single person or a group of people. 

The audience may be of two types: The controlled and the uncontrolled. 

Message 

A message is the information which the communicator transmits to his audience to receive, understand, 
accept & act upon. 

Transmitting the right message to the right people at the right time is a crucial factor in successful 
communications. 

A Good message must be in line with the objective, meaning full, specific & acurate, interesting, culturally 
and socially appropriate etc. 

Channels of Communications 

Channel means "Physical bridge" or the media of communication between the sender & the receiver. 

This Media Systems: 
(i) Interpersonal communication 

(ili) Mass media 

1 (iii) Traditional or folk media

Iti s the flow of information from the audience to the sender; it is the reaction of the audience to the message:|

Types of Communications 

(i) One way Communication (Didactic Method) 

e Flow of communication is one way - from communicator to audience. 

e Drawbacks of didactic method are 

| > Knowledge is imposed 

> Learning is authoritative 

>» Little audience participation 

> No feedback . 

> Does not influence human behaviour 
e Examples 

» Lecture method (Chalk & talk method) 
» Television, Radio, Newsprint 

(ii) © Two way Communication (Socratic Method) 
Two way communicationi n which both the communication and the audience take part. 

e Advantage of two way communication 
>» Active participatory & democratic process 
>» More likely to influence human behaviour 

e Examples 
> Panel discussion 
> Symposium 
> Focus group discussion (FGD)

Formal and Informal Communication 

e Communication has been classified into formal (follows lines of authority) and informal (grape vine) 

communication. 

« Informal network (eg : gossip circles ) exists in all organization. 

Visual Communication

_ |Barriers of Communications 

e Communication Barriers between the educator and the community are 

+ Physiological - Difficulties in hearing, expression. 

+ Environmental - Noise, invisibilities, congestion 

‘| + Psychological - Emotional disturbance, neurosis, level of intelligence, language or 

comprehension difficulties 

+ Cultural - Illiteracy, level of knowledge & understanding customs, beliefs, 

economic & social class differences, religion, attitude etc. 

+ Technical Barriers - Inappropriate selection of media, inadequate projection, concealment 

: =f of information etc.

HEALTH EDUCATION 
CM 4.2 

Definition 

Health education is defined as "a process aimed at encouraging people to want to be healthy, to know how to 

stay healthy, to do what they can individually and collectively to maintain health, and to seek help when needed". 

« The definition adopted by John M last is "the process by which individuals and groups of people learn to 

behave in a manner conducive to the promotion, maintenance or restoration of health". 

e Health education is the part of health care that is concerned with promoting healthy behaviour. 

Changing Concepts 

Modification of individual behaviour — | Modification of social environment 
Community Participation > Community involvement. Promotion of 

individual & community self reliance 
Aims and Objectives 

Aims & Objectives of health education are 

To encourage people to adopt and sustain health Promoting lifestyles and practices

problems. 

+ Make health and health related information easily accessible to the community. 

+ Indicate to the people alternative solution for solving the health & health related problems they nays 

identified. 

+ People must have access to proven preventive measures. 

Approach to Health Education cM 4.3 
\ 

There are 4 well known approach to health education : 

(i) Regulatory approach (Managed prevention) 

(ii) Service approach 

(iii) Health education approach 

(iv) Primary health care approach 

e Regulation Approach 

+  Itis defined as any governmental intervention, direct or
 indirect, designed to alter human behaviour. 

+ Regulation may take many forms ranging from prohibition to imprisonment. 

+ The regulatory approach seeks change in health behaviour an
d improvement in health through a 

variety of external control or laws placed on people. 

Example : The child marriage Restraint Act in India 

Compulsory seat belts in the western countries. 

+ Laws may be useful in times of emergency or in limited situations such as cont
rol of an epidemic 

disease or management of fairs & festivals. 

+ The reason for failure of the regulatory approach are : 

(i) The cause of disease cannot be eradicated by legislation, at the most the government c
an make 

laws to prevent a person spreading disease in his community. 

(ii) In areas involving personal choice, no government can pass legislation t
o force people to eat a 

balanced diet or not to smoke. 

° Service Approach 

+ It aimed at providing all the health services needed by the people at their door steps on the assumption 

that people would use them to improve their own health. 

+ This approach proved a failure because it was not based on the felt needs of the people 

° Health Education Approach 
*+ There are many problems (Eg : cessation of smoking, use of safe water supply, fertility enotrol) which 

can be solved only through health education. 
/ 

+ Since attitude and behavioural patterns are formed early in life; we must move back in time and start 

health education with young population. 
\ 

° Primary Health Care Approach 

+ This is a radically new approach starting from the people with their full participation & active} 
involvement in the planning & delivery of health services based on principles of primary health care, cad

Practices of Health Education 

(i) Audio-Visual Acids 

e No health education can be effective without audiovisual aids. 

e They help to simplify unfamiliar concepts; bring about understanding where words fail; rei
nforce 

learning by appealing to more than one sense, and provide a dynamic way 
of avoiding monotomy. 

e Auditory aids : Radio, Tape-recorder, microphone, earphones etc. 

€ Visual aids: 

(a) Not requiring projection : Chalk board, leaflets, posters, charts, models etc. 

(b) Requiring Projection : Slides, film strips. 

e Combines A-v aids : Television, sound films (Cinema), slide tape combination.

Group Approach s 
The choice of subject in group health teaching is very important it 

must relate directly to the ae ofthe 

group. |: 

(i) repecset and Talk (Lecture) 

For effective communication through lecture method: 

> Group size should be < 30. 

>» Talk duration < 15-20 minutes 

> Combine with flip charts, flannel graphs, exhibits, films and charts. 

Advantages : 

>» Most economical method 

> Less preparation & minimal resources 

> Information transfer in a short time to a large group. 
f 

Disadvantages 

> Learning is passive; does not motivate; suitable only for small groups. 

(ii) Demonstration 

It is carefully planned presentation to show how to perform a skill or procedure. 

Demonstrator carries out step by step in front of an audience and involves them. 

Advantages : 

>» Dramatize by arousing interest . 

> Persuades onlooker to adopt practices 

> Upholds principles of 'seeing is believing’ & learning by doing. 

Uses 

> Environmental sanitation (use of sanitation latrines) 
> MCH (ORS technique) | 
>» Control of disease (scabies) 

(iii) Discussion Methods 

They are as follows, 

(a) Group Discussion 

A Well Conducted A Dominated 3 
Group Discussion Group Discussion \\ 

— 



$n aa 

A group is an aggregation of people interacting in face to face situation. 

For effective group discussion 

>» Group should comprise 6-12 members 

prevent side conve
rsation, 

>» 1group leader (to initiate discussion, help discussion ina proper manner, 

encourage everyone to participate & sums up the discussion) 

>» 1recorder (to record, report on issues discussed and agreements reached). 

Rules to be followed 

>» Listen to what others are saying 

> Express idea clearly & concisely 

> Do not interrupt when others are saying 

> Accept criticism gracefully 

> Help to reach conclusion. 

Advantages 

>» Effective method of health communications 

>» Permits learning by free exchange of ideas, knowledge and opinion. 

> Provides a wider interaction among members. 

Limitations 

> Those shy may not take part in discussion. 

> Some may deviate from subject & make discussion unprofitable. 

(b) Symposium:  Features of a symposium 

A series of speeches / lectures on a selected subject. 

Each person or expert presents an aspect briefly 

There is no discussion among symposium members 

Audience may raise questions in the end 

The chairman makes a comprehensive summary at the end of symposium. 

Advantages 

> Audience remains alert (frequent change of speakers) 

> Good tool for integrated teaching 

(c) Panel Discussion 

Features of a panel discussion 

>» 4-8 persons who are qualified to talk about the topic sit and discuss a given problem / topic in front 
of a target group or audience. 

Panel comprise, a chairman or moderator and 4-8 expert speakers. 

The chairman introduces topic briefly and invites panel members to speak. 

There is no specific agenda, no order of speakin&g no set speeches. 

After speakers explore the topic, audience are invited to take part.

>» Benefit a small number of people.

>» Situation is dramatized by a group

of leaders. 1s 

e Advantages 

>» Useful to discuss problems of human relationships. 

> Useful educational device for school children 

3. Mass Approach (Education of General Public) 

e Mass Media are a "one-way" communication 

e They are useful in transmitting messages to people even in the remotest places. 
e Mass Media alone are generally inadequate in changing human behaviours 

e Methods of Mass Media 

(i) Television 

e Most popular of all Media 

e Creates awareness, influence public opinions & introduce new ways of life. 
e _ Raise levels of understanding 

e Not much opportunity for feedback and discussion. 



Radio 
id | e Purely didactic medium 

e — Valuable aid in putting across health information 
(iii) Internet 

e Fast growing communication media 

e Holds very large potential to become a major health education tool. 
¢ Vast amount of health related literature from WHO and other health agencies is available on line. 

(iv) Newspaper 

e Most widely disseminated of all forms of literature 

e © News-paper have limitation of having low readership in rural areas because of illiteracy. 

e They reach only a limited group i.e., the literates in the community. 

(v) Printed Material : (Magazines, Pamphlets, Booklets) 
e Can convey detailed information 

e Produced in bulk at low cost, can be shared. 

(vi) Direct Mailing 

6 New innovative in health communication 

e These are sent directly to village leaders, literate person, panchayats and local bodies and others who 
are considered as opinion leaders. 

(vii) Posters, Billboards and Signs 

e Can be displayed at public places 

e Less effective in changing behaviour 

e Posters are not expensive when one considers they are seen by a large number of people. 
| (viii) Health museums and exhibitions 

(ix) Folk media.

The correct answer is (b) Panel discussion 

° Panel discussion 

4 -8 subject experts talk about a defined topic in front of a target group of audience 

- Panel comprises of moderator and 4 - 8 speakers 

- There is no’specific order of speaking or no set speeches 

- Audience can also take part in the discussion

PLANNING CYCLE-  - 

Definition 

s assessing the extent to
 

Planning may be defined as a process of analyzing a ystem, or defining a problem, ‘fied 
which the problem exists as a need, formulating goals and objectives to alleviate or ameliorate those pie 2 
needs, examining and choosing from among alternative intervention strategies, initiating the necenee achon ve 

its implementatiaonnd monitoring to ensure proper implementation and evaluating the results of intervention 
In 

the light of stated objective. 

Steps of Planning Cycle 

(i) Analysis of the Health Situation 

+ It involves collection, assessment and interpretation of information to provide a clear pictur
e of health 

———— — a 

situation. ; e

+ Minimum essential data required for health planning. 

- Population, age and sex structures 

~ - Statistics of morbidity and mortality 

ay” — Epidemiology & distribution of disease 

al - Medical health care facilities, Manpower, Training facilities. 

~- Attitude & belief of population 
ee 
(ii) Establishment of Objectives & Goals 

: + Objectives must be established at all levels, down to the smallest organization unit. 

+ At upper level, objectives are general; at successively lower levels, they become more specified and 
detailed. 

+ Objectives may be short term or long term. 

(iii) Assessment of Resources 

+ Resources implies the manpower, money, material, skills, knowledge, and techniques needed for 
implementation of health programme. 

+ Resources are assessed and a balance struck between what is required and what is available. 

fiv)) Fixing Priorities 
+ In fixing priorities, attention is paid to financial constraints, Mortality and Morbidity data, disease which 

can be prevented at low cost, savtihe nlivges of younger people in whom there has been considerable social 
investment; and also political and community interest & pressures. 

—
ia 

iy Write up of Formulated Plan 

+ The plan must be complete in all respects for the execution of a project. 
a Each stage of plan is defined and coasted and the time needed to implement is specified. 
+ The plan must contain working guidance to all those responsible for execution. 
+ It must also contain a built-in system for evaluation.

PANCHAYAT RAJ 

The panchayat Raj is a 3 tier structure of rues local self government in india, linking the village to tt he 
district. 

The three institutions are: 

(i) Panchayat - at village level 

(ii) Panchayat samiti - at block level 

(iii) Zilla parishad - at district level 

The panchayati Raj institutions strengthen democracy at its root, and ensure more effective andt 
participation of the peoplei n the government. 

At village level: 

+ Itconsists of: 

(a) Gram sabha 

(b) Gram panchayat 

(c) Nyaya panchayat 

+ Gram Sabha: It considers proposals for taxation, discuss the annual programme and elect memvpe of 
the gram panchayat. , 3 

+ Gram Panchayat:It strength varies from 15 to 30, and population covered varies from 5,000 to 15,000 of 
more. Members of the panchayat hold office for a period of 3 - 4 years. | 

Function: Cover the entire field of civic administration, including sanitation and public health an | 
socio and economic development of village. 

—— ——— 



hime 

| 

| ; fa It consists of 100 villages and a population of about 80,000 to 1,20,000.

Institutions/ agencies which pool resources from multiple donors (government 
as well as non-government) and often technical and commodity assistance

assistance, or loans. Be Beith ai

Objectives

THE ROCKEFELLER FOUNDATION 

The Rockefeller foundation was founded on 14th may 1913. 

Its purpose is to promote the well being of mankind throughout the world.
 

The foundation's programme included the training of competent teachers
 and research workers; training 

abroad of candidates from India through fellowships, providing grants in

(a) Avoid - Fried food, fat, oil, sugar rich foods

(b) Use unsaturated fat like sunflower, safflower, ground nut;

cereal (maida, noodles, pasta, cakes) and starchy food (potato chips, wafers, french fries)

Potassium < 6g/day 
Sugar < 50 gms 
Carbohydrates: 45 - 65% 
Fat: 20 - 25% 
Protein: 10 - 35% 
Cholesterol: 200 mg/day through restriction of fats & red meat

Treatment Plan

3. ADVICE 

| (Preventive, Promotive and Curative) 

| To Patient 
Take drugs regularly and completely 
Cover the mouth with cloth while coughing 
Take good food, do regular walking/exercise 

Avoid indiscriminate spiting 

Stop smoking 

To Family Members 

Motivate to take drugs regularly and completely, going for periodic examination 
Motivate to take good food, do regular walking/exercise 
Helping for hygienic disposal of sputum

1. A circular well of 10 meter diameter with 15 meter depth of water is to be chlorinated. Horrocks tes? 

shows blue color in 3rd cup onwards. Calculate the quantity of bleaching power (CaOCl_) required to 
disinfect the well ? Explain the procedure of well disinfection? 

‘Solution: 

| Step 1: Finding the volume of the well water 
Volume of water in the circular well =n x r? x h x 1000 

Where, x= decal 
| 7 

| =n*1?xh x 1000 r= radius = 5 mt (half of the diameter) 
= 3.14 x 5°x 151000 h=height = 15 mt water column 

| = 3.14 x 25 x 15 * 1000 1000 = Volume of water per 1 m° 
Volume of water in the well is = 1,177, 500 liter 

‘Step 2: Finding the amount of bleaching powder requirement 

3rd cup is earliest cup showing blue color | ae

ee eee

\+ + Required amount of bleaching powder is mixed with little water in a bucket (not more than 100 gm at a time) 
| to make thin paste 

3/4th of the same bucket is filled with water, stirred well, allowed 10 minute for sedimentation 

|* supernatant clear chlorine solution is transferred to another bucket; lime CaO sediment is discarded. Not 
| poured into the well because sediment increases hardness of the water 
\+ Bucket is lowered into the well below the water level 
+ Well Weer'ls violently agitated by lowering and drawing movements for homogeneous mixing of chlorine 

solution in water 
+ This completes chlorination of well] 
+ ae chlorine should be tested after half an hour, by orthotolidine arsenite test. It should be at least 0.5 

mg/liter 
|

+ Wells are best disinfected once in a week at night 
+ During epidemic wells 
la should be disinfected every day) .

Step 3: Maintaining swimming pool sanitation 

| + People suffering from skin disease, sore eye, nasal or ear discharge, upper respiratory, GI infections and ar 
| communicable disease should not be allowed to swim. 

+ Swimmers are instructed to empty the bladder, bowel and to take shower bath before entering the Doo 
They should not spit blow the nose, urinate and defecate in the pool. 

+ Surrounding environment of the pool should be maintained well. 

| + Pool is cleaned once in 15 day. Water is changed frequently or best subjected for continuous purification, 

+ Pool water is frequently tested for any contamination. 25 sq ft area is provided per swimmer.

Common Problems Faced in Public Health and their Solutions 
the line of management. 

1. Ina boarding school, 14 out of 50 students are suffering from scabies. Discuss 

Solution : 

Diagnosis of disease by 

Complaint : Itching, worse at night 
Management 

Confirmation of the diagnosis 

Microscopic demonstration of itch mite. 
Treatment : 

Secondary infections is treated promptly 
All residents are treated simultaneously (Blanket treatment) 
First, all infested inmates are given a good scrub bath 
After bath, 25 percent benzyl benzoate (sarcopticide) is applied all over the body, below the chin. Allowed to 
dry 
Application is repeated after 12 hour 
Through bath is given 12 hour after the second application 
All under clothes, towels, bedsheets and linens should be boiled, washed, sun dried and ironed. 

Prevention 

Taking bath daily, washing the clothes regularly. 
Maintaining personal hygiene 
Improving the standard of living conditions 
Prevention of overcrowding 
Avoiding sharing the fomites like clothes, bedsheets, etc. 
Avoiding contact with scabies person 
Prompt early diagnosis and treatment 
Health education regarding the cause and prevention of scabies.

Validity of epidemiological indices 

Prevalence of infection 

High coverage of BCG - Interferes with identificati
on of true prevalence 

Cross sensitivity by a typical mycobacteria ~ Over estimate f he pre
valence 

revalence of positive reactors. Age - Specific prevalence is far superior indictor than mere p 

Prevalence of disease (case rate} 

| It is a best index to estimate the case load. 

Age specific prevalence is much more relevant index. 

Incidence of infection 

Reflects the risk of infection in the community 

It expresses the attack force of TB 
It is the best indicator for evaluating the problem and trend of infection 

Incidence of new cases (disease) 

It reflects the new cases, but not total case load 

Reveals the impact of programme as well as the direction of the disease 

Difficult to measure unless case detection is high and reliable 

Inference 

All the indicators should be considered together in interpreting the tuberculosis situation of a community

if answer is ‘yes’ to any question :

Presumptive 

To all suspected cases 
Day 1 - Chloroquine 600 mg single dose + Primaquine 45 mg 
Day 2 - Chloroquine 600 mg

Children 10 mg/kg body weight chloroquine 

0.79 mg/kg body weight primaquine 
Radical 

After microscopic confirmation 
p. vivax — Tablet primaquine - 15 mg daily for 5 day 
p. falciparum — Not required 

Resistant Cases — Chloroquine resistant falciparum 
| Artesunate - 4 mg/kg body weight daily for first 3 day + 

Combination of sulphadoxine - 25 mg/kg and Pyrimethamine - 1.25 mg/kg single dose on first day 
(1500 mg + 75 mg) 
Primaquine - not given for pregnant mother, infants, G, deficiency, with ACT 

Severe, Complicated and Unconscious Cases 
Refer to the higher center and hospitalize where the best facilities are available 
Quinine dihydrochloride 10 mg/per kg weight IV injection in 5% dextrose run over 4 hour 
Kepeat every 8th hour till patient regains consciousness 
Switch over to oral drugs after gaining consciousness 
OR 

| Mefloquine ~ 750 mg for those resistant to chloroquine and ring stage of falciparum

7. Ina town with 1 lakh population, about 20 children were admitted for high fever during first week of

Discuss how do you investigate and manage this problem. 

Solution : 
Given clinical illness makes us think about the possibility of a dengue epidemic. 

Hospital cases represent only the tip of an iceberg. Large number of cases are hidden in the community. This 
should be explored by Epidemiological investigations,

Case definition : Dengue hemorrhagic fever (DHF) 
To define DHF, there should be fever (biphasic) lasting for 2 to 7 day plus two or more other symptoms plus | 
any one lab test positive

Positive tourniquet test Petechiae, ecchymoses, purpura 
Bleeding from mucosa, GIT Hematemesis or melena 

Thrombocytopenia manifestations (at least one of the following) 
Evidence of plasma leakage (due to increase in vascular permeability). 
Kise in the hematocrit > 20 percent for age a nd sex

10. Ina village, untrained dai conducted delivery on a primigravida. Baby, who was normal up to a week, 

| could not take feeds, developed convulsions, spasm of limbs. Discuss the problem and outline the 

management. 

Solution : 

| From case history, it is understood that the baby is suffering from neonatal tetanus. 

Information collected 

‘Epidemiological information : 
| 

| Neonatal tetanus in the district / 1000 live births 
TT coverage in pregnant mothers percent 

___ Proportion of clean deliveries by trained person percent 
| Neonatal tetanus in male child 
| (Total number of cases in the district is considered to be twice the reported number of male cases) 

Neonatal tetanus reporting (to the district medical officer) system in the hospitals. 
Surveillance system at district level : 

_  jClassification of district according to risk of tetanus 

Particulars ; Classification | 
High risk | Control | Low risk | 

Incidence of neonatal tetanus per 1000 livebirths >] 0.1-1 < (0.1 | 

TT 2 dose coverage < 70% 70-90 % > 90% 
Delivery by trained person < 50% 50-75% > 75% 

| Management 
The child is immediately admitted to district hospital/referral center for treatment. 

Passive and active immunization 
(Best given within 6 hours of brith) 

Passive 
Tetanus hyperimmunoglobulin (TIG) 250 IU to 500 IU is given by IM route. 
Tetanus antitoxin neutralizes the circulating toxins, but not the toxin already fixed to the nerve roots. Passive. 
protection is up to 30 days only. | 
If human TIG is not available. | 
Antitetanus serum (ATS) given subcutaneously 1,500 IU after test dose. | 
It gives passive protection for 7 day 

Active 
Along with human hyperimmunoglobulin 

First dose of 0.5 ml tetanus toxoid is given to an other site. | 
6 week later, second dose of tetanus toxoid is given.v 
1 year later, third dose is given. Regular vaccination is continued. 

‘General Treatment and Supportive Measures 

The child is nursed in well-lighted (to observe the changes), but qulet room. 

IM injections and handling of child is minimized as it will provocate spasm. | 

Maintenance of airway-sucking secretions, oxygen is given, if necessary. 
| 

Nutrition (oral feeding is stopped) intravenous/nasogastric tube feeding is given. 
: 

Fluid, electrolyte and temperature is maintained. 

Control of spasm - diazepam IV 0.5 to 1 mg/kg every 3 to 4 hour. 

Antibiotic - penicillin. 
Tracheostomy, assisted ventilation - if indicated.

12. On March 20th 2009, an outbreak of A/HINI influenza occurred in Mexico. By 29th April neighboring 

nine countries have reported 148 confirmed cases including seven deaths. WHO has declared if as 

pandemic imminent (phase 5). Outline the measures to be taken in the country. 

Solution : 
India is a influenza A/H1N1 receptive area 
Population of the country is susceptible to A/H1N1 virus 
Infection can enter through clinical/subclinical travelers 
I. nternational heal th regulations of WHO are adopted to restrict the entry and spread of infection 

| Strict aerial and marine traffic re gulation is undertaken. 
‘Early detection and treatment 

Screening of all travelers at airport 
Co-et raverleleerrss  ofof  thee es uspectsA ma re ; nis 

identified and screened. 
Defining the disease 

| Individuals with positive test for influenza A/HIN1 
Clinical compatible illness or having dies with unexplained acute respiratory illness linked to be probable or 
confirmed case. 

Clinical description 

Acute febrile (> 38 °C) respiratory illness, ranging from influenza like illness to pneumonia. 
| bos Laboratory confirmation 

Sample has to be flown by air to National Institute of Virology (NIV), Pune or National Institute of 
Communicable Diseases (NICD), New Delhi. 
Confirmation tests : 
Real time RT-PCR 
Viral culture 

| Four-fold increase in swine influenza (A/H1N1) virus neutralizing antibodies 
'Case management 

Isolation 

Drug (treatment) : Tamiflu - Recommended only for confirmed cases 
In non-confirmed cases : Tamiflu is not administered, as the drug has serious side effects 
Every case of influenza or pneumonia is rigorously isolated 

| Rapid containment measures are adopted 
Reviewing and revising pandemic plans by periodic comprehensive assessment 
Vaccination : No vaccine available, human seasonal flu vaccine will not give any protection. 

Assessment of situation 

| Rapid but detailed investigation by epidemiological experts 
Assessment of disease : Virological, epidemiological, clinical 
Geographic analysis : Trend, spread, intensity, impact. 

Health education, awareness and advice 

Given throughout the country through mass media 

Reassurance and relieving anxiety is essential as people will be panic during the pandemic 

Preventing exposure to pigs and infected humans 

Do not shake hands, hug or kiss socially 

Do not practice indiscriminate spitting, coughing, sneezing, hawking in public places 

Cover mouth and nose with handkerchief while talking coughi
ng or sneezing 

Wash hands frequently, thoroughly with soap and water, before and after touching nose, mouth,
 eyes 

Avoid overcrowded places 

Work, sleep in a ventilated room 

Drink potable water, eat nutritious food, manage stress
, avoid alcohol 

Stay at home and limit contact with people

Routine surveillance for Influenza like illness (ILI) and severe acute respiratory illness (SARI) 

Changes in phases of pandemic

13. Ina rural primary school, large number of children are having Bitot's spots. What advice can you give oe 

for managing the problem ? 

Solution : 
Bitot's spot denotes vitamin 'A’ deficiency : 
Managing steps are the following. 

immediate measures 

Early diagnosis and treatment. 

| Diagnosis 

All children of the school are examined for the evidence of Bitot's spots and other manifestations of vitamin 'A' 
deficiency. 

Treatment 

Immediately after diagnosis : 
Massive dose of vitamin 'A' (2 lakh IU > 1 year, 1 lakh IU <1 year) is given orally for 2 day © 
One more dose is given 4 week later. 
In case of persistent vomiting or diarrhea, water miscible vitamin A is given by IM. 

Long-term measures 

Health promotion 
| 

Promotion of vitamin ‘A' rich foods | 

Regular and adequate intake of retinol / vitamin 'A' rich foods like dark green leafy vegetables, carrot, drumstick | 
leaves, manog, papaya, milk, egg, butter, oil, fish, etc. Children require 400 to 600 u g of vitamin A per day | 
Promotion of breastfeeding 
Control of infections (that precipitates vitamin A deficiency) like 

Respiratory infections 
Measles 
Diarrhea. 

‘Specific protection 

To prevent occurrence and recurrence of vitamin 'A’ deficiency, vitamin 'A' prophylaxis is given 
National vitamin ‘a’ prophylaxis (under CSSM) 

Scheduler : 9 lakh IU in 5 oral dose

Calculation of iron requirement in mg 

= Normal Hb - patient Hb x weight in kg x 2.21 + 1000 
= (12 gm/ dl - 7.5) x 50 x 2.21 + 1000 
= 4.5 x 50 x 2.21 + 1000 
= 497.25 + 1000 

= 1497.25 mg, to round up 1500 mg 

Iron requirement is 1,500 mg

17. In a school, 8 children are suffering from severe sore throat and fever. How do you iinnvveessttiigg ate and 

manage the situation ? 

Solution : 
Above situation suggests the possibility of rheumatic fever in school children. 

Clinical history and examination of all school children 

|. Fever for long duration with profuse sweating 
2. Joint pain in: Knee, ankle, elbow, wrist 
3, Pain coming spontaneously and subsiding quickly, 
4. Cardiac changes : 

ECG changes - first degree AV block Cardiac enlargement 
Tachycardia Pericarditis 
Cardiac murmurs Cardiac failure 

oF Nodules : Small, painless, non-tender nodules below the skin 
Nodules appear and disappear. 

Laboratory examination 

Throat swab for 

Grams stain 

Culture 

Diagnosis 

Presence of two major manifestations of jones criteria 
or 
One major + Two minor criteria + Evidence of Group A streptococcal infection. 
Major criteria Carditis Erythema marginatum 

Polyarthritis Subcutaneous nodules 
Chorea 

Minor criteria Fever 
Polyarthralgia 
Elevated ESR 

Supporting evidence ECG Prolonged PR interval 
Elevated antistreptolysin-O 
Positive throat culture 

Management 

Primary prevention Single IM injection 12 lakh unit (6 lakh unit for children) of Benzathine Pencicillin 
(to prevent the first attack) (After test dose) 

Secondary prevention Benzathine penicillin 12 lakh unit (6 lakh unit for children) once in a month 
(to prevent recurrence) For at least 5 year or until child reaches 18 year whichever is later 

If there is second attack, prophylaxis is for life time 
Erythromycin prophylaxis (If person is allergic to penicillin). 

Preventive measures 

¢ Improving the living standard 
* Periodic throat swab culture examination of school children for early diagnosis and treatment 
¢ Prevention of recurrence by penicillin prophylaxis. 

Surveillance 

Periodic surveillance of school age (5 to 14 year) children at schools and slums at 5 year interval.

. Median ais t he middle (central) value, after arranging all values in an ascending or descending order. It

Merits: | Median is more representative central value and more nearer to the truth than mean 

Can be calculated in a grouped series 
Not distorted by extreme values 
In a series with extreme value the median is most representative. 

Demerits ; Cannot be calculated by raw data with out arrangement. 
Range cannot be understood 
Cannot be plotted graphically 
The median is not used as central in analytic calculation 
The median of different series (median of median) cannot be combined,

9,7, 8, 10, 73, 5, 6, 4, 11, 12 

In this series, there is no mode, because here there is no repeatedly occurring value (A series may have no

‘Merits: Easy to obtain and useful in epidemiological work 

Not affected by extreme values. 

‘Demerit: Need not be a central value 

No analytic concepts are based on the mode

| Coefficient of variation is the ratio of the standard deviation of series to the mean of series.

8.4 
= 77 *100 

Co-efficient of variation = 11.6%

Range is the difference between highest and lowest figures in series. 
Highest is 86 

Lowest is 59 
Range 59 to 86

Validity of rates 

Crude birth rate Unsatisfactory measure, since all population is not taking part in child bearing 

General fertility rate: Better measure than crude birth rate, because it is restricted to female 
population of child bearing age, However, not all women are exposed to 
pregnancy. 

Total fertility rate Gives average number of children a women would have, if sh e passes the 

same fertility pattern. It gives a picture of complete family size. |
